(91)

We focus on the three-point amplitude
A^{15P_{1}}(q_{1})
, which is related to the four-point amplitude via the tree-level unitarity (
88
).
In this relation we have a sum over a basis of possible polarizations
\epsilon_{5}
,
which can be evaluated using completeness relations, e.g. for massive bosons
Boels:2014dka

\sum\limits_{\epsilon_{5}}\epsilon_{5}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{|\rho|}}\epsilon_{5}^{\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{|\rho|}}=P^{\mu_{1}}_{5\gamma_{1}}\ldots P^{\mu_{\rho}}_{5\gamma_{\rho}}\pi_{\rho}^{\gamma_{1}\ldots\gamma_{|\rho|};\,\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{|\rho|}}P^{\nu_{1}}_{5\sigma_{1}}\ldots P^{\nu_{\rho}}_{5\sigma_{\rho}}\,,

(92)

where
\pi_{\rho}
is the projector to the irreducible
SO(D-1)
representation
\rho
and

P_{5\nu}^{\mu}=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}-\frac{k_{5}^{\mu}k_{5\nu}}{k_{5}^{2}}\,,

(93)

is a projector to the space transverse to
k_{5}
.
We will always absorb the projectors
P_{5\nu}^{\mu}
into the three-point amplitudes, i.e. consider amplitudes in a transverse configuration. That means that the indices corresponding to particle 5 have to be constructed from the projected momenta of the other particles, which are identical

P_{5\nu}^{\mu}l_{1\mu}=P_{5\nu}^{\mu}k_{1\mu}\,.

(94)

Apart from that, massive particles can also have a longitudinal polarization
v
which satisfies

v\cdot k_{5}=0\,,\qquad v^{2}=1\,,

(95)

and is given in this frame explicitly by

v_{\mu}=\frac{1}{m_{5}}\left(k_{5}^{u},-\frac{m_{5}^{2}}{k_{5}^{u}},0\right)\,.

(96)

For the case that particle 1 is a scalar, we can then construct
A^{15P}
in terms of the following manifestly transverse tensor structures

A^{15P}_{m_{5},\rho_{5},\bm{\mu}}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{|\rho_{5}|}a_{m_{5},\rho_{5}}^{k}(t_{1})\,i^{|\rho_{5}|}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}^{|\rho_{5}|-k}v_{\mu_{1}}\ldots v_{\mu_{k}}q_{1\mu_{k+1}}\ldots q_{1\mu_{|\rho_{5}|}}\,,

(97)

where we introduced boldface indices
\bm{\mu}
as multi-indices that stand for the
|\rho|
indices for the irrep
\rho
. By abuse of language we defined the vector
q_{1}\equiv(0,0,q_{1})
, since
q_{1}
is transverse.
If particle 1 carries spin as well, as will be the case for the gravitons considered later on, we construct the polarization tensors out of the vector
\xi_{1}=(\xi_{1}^{u},\xi_{1}^{v},\epsilon_{1})
. In this case, again defining

\epsilon_{1}\equiv(0,0,\epsilon_{1})
, the amplitudes take the following form in the Regge limit

\displaystyle A^{15P}_{m_{5},\rho_{5},\bm{\mu}}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\ell_{1}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{|\rho_{5}|-n}

\displaystyle a_{m_{5},\rho_{5}}^{k,n}(t_{1})\,i^{|\rho_{5}|}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}^{|\rho_{5}|+\ell_{1}-2n-k}(\epsilon_{1}\cdot q_{1})^{\ell_{1}-n}

(98)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1\mu_{1}}\ldots\epsilon_{1\mu_{n}}v_{\mu_{n+1}}\ldots v_{\mu_{n+k}}q_{1\mu_{n+k+1}}\ldots q_{1\mu_{|\rho_{5}|}}\,,

as can be checked by comparing with the explicit amplitudes computed in
DAppollonio:2013mgj
. These choices for the tensor structures are particularly convenient since
q_{1}\cdot v=\epsilon_{1}\cdot v=0
. Contact with the momentum frame used in the previous subsections is made by identifying the Lorentz invariant
A^{12P_{1}P_{2}}
.

4
AdS impact parameter space

Our goal in this section is to compute the Regge limit of a scalar four-point function in a perturbative large
N
CFT at one-loop and finite

\Delta_{\text{gap}}
. At finite
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
we have to consider the
t
-channel exchange of all possible double-trace operators and also single-trace operators, which are respectively dual to tidal excitations of the external scattering states and to long-string creation in the string theory context. It was shown in
Meltzer:2019pyl
that the exchange of single-trace operators dual to the long-string creation effects is subleading in the Regge limit. Therefore we only need to consider the exchange of double-trace operators.
This is where the new perturbative CFT optical theorem (
5
) takes a central role, as it allows us to compute the contributions of double-trace operators to the correlator starting from the corresponding tree-level correlators. The contribution of the leading Regge trajectory to the scalar tree-level correlators is known to leading order in the Regge limit
Cornalba:2007fs
;
Costa:2012cb
.

In this section we will therefore study (
5
) in the Regge limit, and this time we expand the tree-level correlators in the
s
-channel. In the Regge limit the four external points are
in Lorentzian kinematics as depicted in figure
3
.

y_{1}
y_{4}
y_{3}
y_{2}
y^{+}
y^{-}

Figure 3:
Kinematics in the central Poincaré patch with coordinates
y_{i}
. Time is on the vertical axis, transverse directions are suppressed.

In this configuration all distances between points are spacelike except for
y_{14}^{2},y_{23}^{2}<0
.
The Regge limit is reached by sending the four-points to infinity along the light cones

y_{1}^{+}\rightarrow-\infty,\quad y_{2}^{+}\rightarrow+\infty,\quad y_{3}^{-}\rightarrow-\infty,\quad y_{4}^{-}\rightarrow+\infty\,.

(99)

The Regge limit can be directly applied to the left hand side of (
5
). The terms on the Regge sheets
A^{\circlearrowleft}(y_{i})
and
A^{\circlearrowright}(y_{i})
are dominant over the Euclidean terms in this limit. However, we cannot apply the Regge limit directly to the right hand side of (
5
) as the shadow integrals range over Euclidean configurations. Hence we will apply Wick rotations on the points
y_{5},\,y_{6},\,y_{7},\,y_{8}
to obtain a gluing of the discontinuities of Lorentzian correlators. We will assume that in the Regge limit the dominant contribution to the gluing formula comes from the domain where the individual tree-level correlators are in the Regge limit themselves. We do not provide a proof of this assumption but we justify it in section
4.1
.

When each four-point function in (
61
) is in the Regge limit, the points
y_{5},\,y_{6},\,y_{7},\,y_{8}
are placed in the same positions as
y_{1},\,y_{4},\,y_{2},\,y_{3}
in fig.
3
, respectively. Thus
y_{7}
is in the future of
y_{8}
and this pair is spacelike from
y_{1},\,y_{4},\,y_{5},\,y_{6}
. Similarly,
y_{6}
is in the future of
y_{5}
and is spacelike from
y_{2},\,y_{3},\,y_{7},\,y_{8}
.
For the chosen kinematics
we put the pair
y_{5},\,y_{6}
in anti-time order using the epsilon prescription of (
58
) with
\epsilon_{5}>\epsilon_{6}
, and the pair
y_{7},\,y_{8}
in time order using
\epsilon_{7}>\epsilon_{8}
. Applying this on (
70
) gives the following formula for the Regge limit of the double discontinuity

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{t}A_{\text{1-loop}}(y_{k})\Big|_{\text{d.t.}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}_{5},\mathcal{O}_{6}}\frac{1}{N_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}N_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}}\int

\displaystyle dy_{5}dy_{6}dy_{7}dy_{8}\,\langle[\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{3}]\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\rangle_{\text{tree}}\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{7}^{{\dagger}}\rangle

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{8}^{{\dagger}}\rangle\langle\mathcal{O}_{7}\mathcal{O}_{8}[\mathcal{O}_{4},\mathcal{O}_{1}]\rangle_{\text{tree}}\Big|_{\left[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\right]}\,.

(100)

The relative ordering between
\epsilon_{5},\epsilon_{7}
and
\epsilon_{6},\epsilon_{8}
is irrelevant as the pairs, appearing in the two-point functions on the right hand side of (
100
), are spacelike separated in the Regge configuration.

In this section we define the discontinuities as the commutators inserted into the fully Lorentzian correlators

\displaystyle\Disc_{14}A^{1874}(y_{i})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\langle\mathcal{O}_{7}\mathcal{O}_{8}[\mathcal{O}_{4},\mathcal{O}_{1}]\rangle=A^{1874}{}^{\circlearrowleft}(y_{i})-A^{1874}_{\text{Euc}}(y_{i})\,,

(101)

\displaystyle\Disc_{23}A^{3652}(y_{i})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\langle[\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{3}]\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\rangle=A^{3652}_{\text{Euc}}(y_{i})-A^{3652}{}^{\circlearrowright}(y_{i})\,.

This definition differs slightly from the one in (
22
). Stripping out the appropriate pre-factors from (
101
), one can check that these single discontinuities can be equivalently defined as

\displaystyle\Disc_{14}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle\coloneqq e^{i\pi(a+b)}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z}^{\circlearrowleft})-e^{-i\pi(a+b)}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z})\,,

(102)

\displaystyle\Disc_{23}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z})-\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z}^{\circlearrowright})\,,

\displaystyle\Disc_{23}\mathcal{A}^{3412}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle=e^{-i\pi(a+b)}\mathcal{A}^{3412}(z,\overline{z})-e^{i\pi(a+b)}\mathcal{A}^{3412}(z,\overline{z}^{\circlearrowright})\,.

Starting from the discontinuity defined in (
22
), these expressions result from continuing another half circle in
\overline{z}
, so that the different terms are either evaluated at the original position or continued a full circle around 1. The extra phase comes from the additional Wick rotations.
The final result matches the definition of the discontinuity in
Caron-Huot:2020nem
.
Note that
\overline{z}
is continued an extra half circle in opposite directions for the first two lines in (
102
), so that with these definitions the relation to the
dDisc
in (
21
) remains valid.
4
4

4

For
t
-channel blocks, the new definitions for the discontinuities in (
102
) are related to the old definition in (
22
) by a phase, for example, for
\Disc_{14}
the relative phase is
e^{i\pi\tau_{\mathcal{O}}/2}
.

Therefore the optical theorem in the Regge limit can still be expressed as

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{t}A_{\rm 1-loop}(y_{i})\Big|_{\text{d.t.}}\!=-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{O}_{5},\mathcal{O}_{6}\\
\in\ s.t.\end{subarray}}\!\int dy_{5}dy_{6}\,\Disc_{23}A_{\rm tree}^{3652}(y_{k})\,\mathbf{S}_{5}\mathbf{S}_{6}\Disc_{14}A_{\rm tree}^{1564}(y_{k})\Big|_{[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}]}\,,

(103)

with the discontinuities as defined in the first and third lines of (
102
), and the gluing and shadow integrals now ranging over Minkowski space. This formula is also depicted in figure
4
in terms of Witten diagrams.

Figure 4:
Optical theorem in the Regge limit in terms of Witten diagrams. The tree-level correlators are dominated by
s
-channel Pomeron exchange. The external operators are scalars, while
\mathcal{O}_{5}
and
\mathcal{O}_{6}
are summed over all states that couple to the
external scalars and the Pomeron (tidal excitations). The ellipses on the l.h.s. indicate that all string excitations are taken into account.

We should also note that for real
z,\overline{z}
,
\Disc_{23}
in the third line of (
102
) is related to
\Disc_{14}
in the first line by

\Disc_{23}\mathcal{A}^{3412}(z,\overline{z})=-\left.\left(\Disc_{14}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z})\right)^{*}\right|_{(a,b)\to(-b,-a)}\,,\quad 0<z,\overline{z}<1\,.

(104)

Applied to the correlators appearing in (
103
) this reads

\Disc_{23}\mathcal{A}^{3652}(z,\overline{z})=-\left.\left(\Disc_{14}\mathcal{A}^{1564}(z,\overline{z})\right)^{*}\right|_{1564\to 3652}\,.

(105)

To benefit from this useful relation, we will always strip out a pre-factor such that we obtain the correlator
\mathcal{A}^{3652}(z,\overline{z})
on the right hand side of (
103
). Otherwise we would have to use the second line of (
102
) for
\Disc_{23}
.
Finally, in the Regge limit the analytically continued correlators are dominant over the Euclidean contributions so that we have

\displaystyle\Disc_{14}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle\approx e^{i\pi(a+b)}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z}^{\circlearrowleft})\,,

(106)

\displaystyle\Disc_{23}\mathcal{A}^{3412}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle\approx-e^{i\pi(a+b)}\mathcal{A}^{3412}(z,\overline{z}^{\circlearrowright})\,,

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{t}\,\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{1}{2}\left(e^{i\pi(a+b)}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z}^{\circlearrowleft})+e^{-i\pi(a+b)}\mathcal{A}^{1234}(z,\overline{z}^{\circlearrowright})\right)\,,

where the
\approx
sign means we took the Regge limit.
In order to account for the tidal excitations, the operators
\mathcal{O}_{5}
and
\mathcal{O}_{6}
can carry spin, in which case their indices are contracted with the ones of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}
and sums over tensor structures are implied. In subsections
4.1
,

4.2
and

4.3
below we will mostly suppress the aspect of spinning correlators. We will come back to this issue in subsection
4.4
.

4.1
Regge limit

To obtain the impact parameter representation, we first change the coordinate system placing each point on a different Poincaré patch as shown in figure
5
. We use the following coordinate transformations

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(x_{i}^{+},x_{i}^{-},x_{i\perp}\right)=-\frac{1}{y_{i}^{+}}\left(1,y_{i}^{2},y_{i\perp}\right),

\displaystyle i=1,2,5,7\,,

(107)

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(x_{i}^{+},x_{i}^{-},x_{i\perp}\right)=-\frac{1}{y_{i}^{-}}\left(1,y_{i}^{2},y_{i\perp}\right),

\displaystyle i=3,4,6,8\,.

x_{1}
x_{4}
x_{3}
x_{2}
\mathcal{P}_{4}
\mathcal{P}_{1}
\mathcal{P}_{3}
\mathcal{P}_{2}

Figure 5:
The external operators at coordinates
x_{i}
in their respective Poincaré patches
\mathcal{P}_{i}
. The black dotted lines are identified when the Poincaré patches are wrapped on the boundary of the global AdS cylinder.

In the new
x_{i}
coordinates, the Regge limit corresponds to placing the four external points at the origin of their respective Poincaré patches,

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\to 0\,.

(108)

However,
x_{5}
to
x_{8}
are integrated over in the CFT optical theorem.

Conformal correlators transform covariantly under the transformation (
107
).
In the scalar case we have

A\left(y_{i}\right)=(-y_{1}^{+})^{-\Delta_{1}}(y_{2}^{+})^{-\Delta_{2}}(-y_{3}^{-})^{-\Delta_{3}}(y_{4}^{-})^{-\Delta_{4}}A\left(x_{i}\right)\,.

(109)

In the spinning case, one must additionally account for the Jacobian matrix
\partial y^{a}/\partial x^{m}
.
5
5

5

For external spinning operators, the conformal transformations have a non-trivial rotation matrix
\partial y^{a}/\partial x^{m}
. Conformal covariance of the correlators gives, in the representative example of two vectors and two scalars
Cornalba:2009ax
,
A^{ab}\left(y_{i}\right)=\left(-y_{1}^{+}y_{2}^{+}\right)^{-1-\Delta_{V}}\left(-y_{3}^{-}y_{4}^{-}\right)^{-\Delta_{S}}\frac{\partial y_{1}^{a}}{\partial x_{1}^{m}}\frac{\partial y_{2}^{b}}{\partial x_{2}^{n}}A^{mn}\left(x_{i}\right)
. These matrices ensure that the inversion tensors are correctly mapped from
y_{i}
to
x_{i}
variables, preserving their form.

Next we use conformal symmetry to express the correlator in terms of two vectors.
This is similar to expressing the correlator in terms of two scalar cross-ratios, with the difference that here we fix two, instead of the customary three, positions using translations and special conformal transformations to express the correlator in terms of the remaining two position vectors.
We can follow
Cornalba:2009ax
and use a translation to send
x_{1}
to 0
which, due to the different transformations in (
107
) (see also
Kulaxizi_2018
), will act as a special conformal transformation on the Poincaré patches for
x_{3}
and
x_{4}
,

x_{1}\to 0\,,\quad x_{2}\to x_{2}-x_{1}\,,\quad x_{3,4}\to\frac{x_{3,4}-x_{3,4}^{2}x_{1}}{1-2x_{3,4}\cdot x_{1}+x_{3,4}^{2}x_{1}^{2}}\,.

(110)

Next we implement a translation on the
x_{3}
and
x_{4}
patches (acting as special conformal transformation on
x_{1,2}
) to also map
x_{4}
to 0 in its own patch and find that the correlator as a function of the Poincaré patch coordinates
A(x_{i})
, as defined in (
109
), can always be expressed as

A(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\approx A(0,-x,\overline{x}/\overline{x}^{2},0)\equiv A(x,\overline{x})\,,

(111)

with

x\approx x_{1}-x_{2}\,,\qquad\overline{x}\approx x_{3}-x_{4}\,,

(112)

in the Regge limit (
108
).

It is further convenient to implement the coordinate change using embedding space coordinates
P^{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{2,d}

P^{M}=\big(P^{+},P^{-},P^{m}\big)\,,\qquad P\cdot P=-P^{+}P^{-}+\eta_{mn}P^{m}P^{n}\,.

(113)

These are related to the coordinates
y^{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
of physical Minkowski space by
Cornalba:2009ax

P^{M}=\big(y^{+},y^{-},1,y^{2},y_{\perp}\big)\quad\Rightarrow\quad P_{ij}\equiv-2P_{i}\cdot P_{j}=(y_{i}-y_{j})^{2}\,,

(114)

and to the coordinates
x_{i}
by

\displaystyle P_{1}^{M}

\displaystyle=-y_{1}^{+}\left(-1,-x_{1}^{2},x_{1}^{m}\right)\,,

\displaystyle P_{2}^{M}=y_{2}^{+}\left(-1,-x_{2}^{2},x_{2}^{m}\right)\,,

(115)

\displaystyle P_{3}^{M}

\displaystyle=-y_{3}^{-}\left(-x_{3}^{2},-1,x_{3}^{m}\right),

\displaystyle P_{4}^{M}=y_{4}^{-}\left(-x_{4}^{2},-1,x_{4}^{m}\right)\,.

One can easily show that the cross-ratios (
26
) are given in terms of
x
and
\overline{x}
as

z\overline{z}=x^{2}\overline{x}^{2}\,,\qquad(1-z)(1-\overline{z})=1+x^{2}\overline{x}^{2}+2x\cdot\overline{x}\,,

(116)

and the kinematic prefactor (
25
) becomes

T^{1234}=\frac{(-y_{1}^{+})^{-\Delta_{1}}(y_{2}^{+})^{-\Delta_{2}}(-y_{3}^{-})^{-\Delta_{3}}(y_{4}^{-})^{-\Delta_{4}}}{x^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}\overline{x}^{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}}}\,.

(117)

When combining (
24
) and (
109
), the numerator of the last expression cancels the Jacobian prefactor in (
109
) to give,

A\left(x_{i}\right)=\frac{A^{1234}(z,\overline{z})}{x^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}\overline{x}^{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}}}\,.

(118)

If we now study the correlator
A^{3652}(x_{i})
, a priori we have to take into account that only
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
are affected by the Regge limit. However, we will assume that the integration will be dominated by the region where the integration points are also boosted. Using the embedding space coordinates

\displaystyle P_{5}^{M}=-y_{5}^{+}\left(-1,-x_{5}^{2},x_{5}^{m}\right)\,,\qquad P_{6}^{M}

\displaystyle=y_{6}^{-}\left(-x_{6}^{2},-1,x_{6}^{m}\right)\,,

(119)

we find

x^{\prime}\approx x_{5}-x_{2}\,,\qquad\overline{x}^{\prime}\approx x_{3}-x_{6}\,.

(120)

Where the primed variables are meant to emphasize that the points
1,4
are replaced by
5,6
in this correlator as compared to (
111
).
Performing these steps for all the correlators in (
103
) we find,

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{t}A_{\text{1-loop}}(x_{12},x_{34})=-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}_{5},\mathcal{O}_{6}}\int dx_{5}dx_{6}\,\Disc_{23}A^{3652}_{\text{tree}}(x_{36},x_{52})

(121)

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{5}\mathbf{S}_{6}\Disc_{14}A^{1564}_{\text{tree}}(x_{15},x_{64})\Big|_{\left[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\right]}\,,

where we stop explicitly mentioning that we are dealing with only the contribution of double-trace operators as single-trace contributions are subleading in the limit considered.
Let us stress that in order to write the correlators on the right hand side in terms of two differences, we assumed that each of the individual tree-level correlators are in the Regge limit themselves.
The easiest way to justify this is in Fourier space using the impact parameter transform defined below.
Each tree-level position space correlator is dominated by a power
\sigma^{1-j(\nu)}
in the Regge limit, which maps to a power of the AdS center of mass energy
S^{j(\nu)-1}
in impact parameter space. Since the optical theorem is multiplicative in impact parameter space, subleading Regge trajectories or kinematical corrections from the conformal block at finite boost get mapped to smaller powers of
S
, and therefore do not contribute to the leading behavior.
The eikonal approximation in AdS
Cornalba:2007zb
gives additional intuition for this, since it means that even in AdS, the particles remain essentially undeflected, scattering forward each time they exchange a Pomeron. Furthermore, we will show in section
5
that this configuration reproduces the behavior at one-loop derived in
Meltzer:2019pyl
.

4.2
Impact parameter space

Let us now consider the two-point functions
\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{5}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{7}\rangle
and
\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{8}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{6}\rangle
for the shadow transforms in (
121
).
In the Regge configuration,
x_{5}
is the patch of
x_{1}
,
x_{6}
is the patch of
x_{4}
,
x_{7}
is the patch of
x_{2}
and
x_{8}
is the patch of
x_{3}
.
As explained in
Cornalba:2007zb
;
Kravchuk:2018htv
, the two-point function between two coordinates on adjacent Poincaré patches has an additional phase factor
e^{i\pi\Delta}
and this phase can be accounted for by switching from the
i\epsilon
prescription of a Feynman propagator to that of a Wightman propagator (see
Cornalba:2007zb
).
The normalization of the shadow transform in (
10
) and (
11
) is obtained from the Fourier transform of a two-point function as the shadow transform acts multiplicatively in Fourier space (see section 3.2 of
Karateev:2018oml
). The normalization in (
11
) is obtained from the Fourier transform of a Euclidean two-point function, which matches the one of a Lorentzian two-point function with Feynman
i\epsilon
prescription. The Wightman propagator in momentum space however has support only on the future lightcone and the coefficient of the Fourier transform is different (see Appendix B of
Cornalba:2007zb
and
section 2.1 of
Gillioz:2018mto
)

\displaystyle\int dx\frac{e^{-2iq\cdot x}}{\left[-(x^{0}-i\epsilon)^{2}+\vec{x}^{2}\right]^{\Delta}}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}}\,\Theta(q^{0})\,\Theta(-q^{2})\left(-q^{2}\right)^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}\,,

(122)

with

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}+1}}{\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma\big(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+1\big)}\,.

(123)

Consequently we change the normalization
N_{\mathcal{O}}
of the shadow transform in (
10
) to (for scalar operators)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{M}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}\,.

(124)

Next we define, following
Cornalba:2007fs
, the impact parameter representation as the Fourier transform of the discontinuity of the correlator in the two remaining vectors
6
6

6

The AdS impact parameter representation was previously defined in
Cornalba:2007fs
;
Cornalba:2006xm
;
Cornalba:2008qf
as the Fourier transform of the correlator on the second sheet
A(z,\overline{z}^{\circlearrowleft})
. Since this contribution dominates in the Regge limit, it is indistinguishable from the discontinuity of the correlator in this limit.
However, in the
t
-channel the two notions are clearly different and it was necessary to take the discontinuity to derive (
5
).
In the next section we will see that the discontinuity is the better choice also in the
s
-channel.

\Disc_{14}A^{1jk4}(x_{1j},x_{k4})=\int dp\,d\overline{p}\,e^{-2ip\cdot x_{1j}-2i\overline{p}\cdot x_{k4}}B^{1jk4}(p,\overline{p})\,,

(125)

where the function
B(p,\overline{p})
has support only on the future Milne wedge of
p
and
\overline{p}
.
Using (
105
) on (
125
) we get the Fourier transform of
\Disc_{23}
.

\displaystyle\Disc_{23}A^{3kj2}(x_{3k},x_{j2})=-\int dp\,d\overline{p}\,e^{-2ip\cdot x_{3k}-2i\overline{p}\cdot x_{j2}}B^{3kj2}(-p,-\overline{p})^{*}\,.

(126)

The causal relations and thus the
i\epsilon
prescription in (
126
) are opposite to those in (
125
) and the complex conjugation prescribed in (
105
) compensates for that.
Inserting (
125
) into (
121
) and using that
A^{1\tilde{5}\tilde{6}4}_{\text{tree}}
is a double shadow transform of
A^{1784}_{\text{tree}}
we obtain, upon using (
105
) for the
\Disc_{23}
,

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{t}A_{\text{1-loop}}(x_{12},x_{34})=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}_{5},\mathcal{O}_{6}}\int dx_{5}\,dx_{6}\,dx_{7}\,dx_{8}\;\int dp\,d{\overline{p}}\,dp^{\prime}\,d\overline{p}^{\prime}

(127)

\displaystyle\times e^{-2i(p^{\prime}\cdot x_{36}+\overline{p}^{\prime}\cdot x_{52}+p\cdot x_{17}+\overline{p}\cdot x_{84})}B^{3652}_{\text{tree}}(-p^{\prime},-{\overline{p}}^{\prime})^{*}B^{1564}_{\text{tree}}(p,{\overline{p}})

\displaystyle\times\frac{T^{(\rho_{5})}(x_{75})}{\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}\left[-(x^{0}_{75}-i\epsilon)^{2}+\vec{x}_{75}^{2}\right]^{d-\Delta_{5}}}\frac{T^{(\rho_{6})}(x_{68})}{\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}\left[-(x^{0}_{68}-i\epsilon)^{2}+\vec{x}_{68}^{2}\right]^{d-\Delta_{6}}}\Bigg|_{\left[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\right]}\,.

T^{(\rho)}(x_{ij})
is the tensor structure for the two-point function of an operator with
SO(d)
quantum number
\rho
. For example it is the familiar inversion tensor
\eta^{\mu\nu}-2\frac{x^{\mu}x^{\nu}}{x^{2}}
for spin 1 operators. Note that we have
B^{1564}_{\text{tree}}
instead of
B^{1784}_{\text{tree}}
, as the superscripts now only indicate the dimensions of the corresponding operators and
\Delta_{5}=\Delta_{7}
,
\Delta_{6}=\Delta_{8}
.

We can now express the two-point functions from the shadow transforms in Fourier space by inverting (
122
)

\displaystyle\frac{T^{(\rho)}(x)}{\left[-(x^{0}-i\epsilon)^{2}+\vec{x}^{2}\right]^{d-\Delta}}={}\frac{\mathcal{M}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}}{\pi^{d}}\int\limits_{M}dq\,e^{-2iq\cdot x}\,\widehat{T}^{(\rho)}(q)\,(-q^{2})^{\frac{d}{2}-\Delta}\,.

(128)

The Fourier space integral is over the future Milne wedge
M
as the Fourier transform has support only on this domain.

\widehat{T}^{(\rho)}(q)
is the tensor structure of the two-point function in Fourier space,
which has been discussed for example in
Gillioz:2018mto
.
It is a tensor composed of
q^{\mu}
and
\eta^{\mu\nu}
that can be factorized into a product of new tensors
t^{(\rho)}(q)
as follows

\widehat{T}^{(\rho)}(q)^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{|\rho|}}_{\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{|\rho|}}=t^{(\rho)}(q)^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{|\rho|}}_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{|\rho|}}t^{(\rho)}(q)^{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{|\rho|}}_{\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{|\rho|}}\,.

(129)

Using (
128
) in (
127
) we end up with four position integrals over
x_{5},x_{6},x_{7},x_{8}
and six integrals over
q,\overline{q}
(from the four-point functions) and
p,\overline{p},p^{\prime},\overline{p}^{\prime}
. The four position integrals give four Dirac delta functions with which we can eliminate the
q,\overline{q},p^{\prime},\overline{p}^{\prime}
integrals to obtain

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{t}A_{\text{1-loop}}(x_{12},x_{34})=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2d}}{2}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}_{5},\mathcal{O}_{6}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}}\int dp\,d{\overline{p}}\,e^{-2i(p\cdot x_{12}+{\overline{p}}\cdot x_{34})}

(130)

\displaystyle\frac{B^{3652}_{\text{tree}}(-{\overline{p}},-p)^{*}\;\,\widehat{T}^{(\rho_{5})}(p)\,\widehat{T}^{(\rho_{6})}(\overline{p})\;\,B^{1564}_{\text{tree}}(p,{\overline{p}})}{(-p^{2})^{\Delta_{5}-\frac{d}{2}}(-\overline{p}^{2})^{\Delta_{6}-\frac{d}{2}}}\Bigg|_{\left[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\right]}\,,

with an implicit index contraction between
B^{1564}_{\text{tree}}
and
B^{3652}_{\text{tree}}
and the tensor structures
\widehat{T}^{(\rho)}
.
At this point we use (
129
) and absorb the
t^{(\rho)}(q)
tensors into the definition of the phase shifts
B_{\text{tree}}
. This means that one needs to take it into account if one wants to relate tensor structures of CFT correlators and phase shifts, but we will not need to do such a basis change explicitly in this work.
Using (
106
) we see that the double discontinuity corresponds to the quantity

-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}B(p,\overline{p})

in impact parameter space.
Thus we find the following gluing formula for the impact parameter representation, which is purely multiplicative,

-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}B_{\text{1-loop}}(p,\overline{p})=\frac{\pi^{2d}}{2}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}_{5},\mathcal{O}_{6}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}}\,\frac{B^{3652}_{\text{tree}}(-{\overline{p}},-p)^{*}\,B^{1564}_{\text{tree}}(p,{\overline{p}})}{(-p^{2})^{\Delta_{5}-\frac{d}{2}}(-\overline{p}^{2})^{\Delta_{6}-\frac{d}{2}}}\Big|_{\left[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\right]}\,.

(131)

Let us consider the case when
\mathcal{O}_{5}=\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{6}=\mathcal{O}_{3}
. In this case, it is useful to strip out a scale factor similar to that in (
118
) from the impact parameter representation

B^{jjkk}(p,\overline{p})=\frac{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{j}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{k}}\;\mathcal{B}^{jjkk}(p,{\overline{p}})}{\left(-p^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-\Delta_{j}}\left(-\overline{p}^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-\Delta_{k}}}\,.

(132)

Using (
122
) one sees that with this choice of normalization the impact parameter representation of the MFT correlator is
\mathcal{B}^{jjkk}_{\text{MFT}}=1
,
which is necessary for the eikonalization of the phase shift in AdS gravity.
Inspired by this fact we choose the normalization

B^{ijkl}(p,\overline{p})=\frac{\sqrt{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{i}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{j}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{k}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{l}}}\;\mathcal{B}^{ijkl}(p,{\overline{p}})}{\left(-p^{2}\right)^{\frac{d-\Delta_{i}-\Delta_{j}}{2}}\left(-\overline{p}^{2}\right)^{\frac{d-\Delta_{k}-\Delta_{l}}{2}}}\,,

(133)

for the general case.
This gives the following compact form for the optical theorem in impact parameter space

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(p,\overline{p})=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\mathcal{O}_{5},\mathcal{O}_{6}}\;\mathcal{B}^{3652}_{\text{tree}}(-{\overline{p}},-p)^{*}\;\mathcal{B}^{1564}_{\text{tree}}(p,{\overline{p}})\;\Bigg|_{\left[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\right]}\,.

(134)

In
Meltzer:2019pyl
, the Regge limit of a one-loop four-point function of scalars was studied in the large
\lambda
regime with
S\gg\lambda\gg 1
. The contribution of tidal excitations to the correlator is suppressed in this regime. It corresponds to just one term in the sum on the right hand side of (
134
) i.e. with
\mathcal{O}_{5}=\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{6}=\mathcal{O}_{3}
. We show in section
5.1
that this term from our formula (
134
) reproduces the result from
Meltzer:2019pyl
in the large
\lambda
or equivalently the large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}^{2}
limit. We do not need to discard any shadow double-trace contributions for this match. This motivates us to assume that the only non-zero contributions to the gluing of tree-level correlators in the Regge limit is from the physical double-traces
[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}]
, and we will drop the explicit projections henceforth. This is compatible with the intuition that there is no need to project out shadow operators in a Lorentzian CFT optical theorem.

4.3
s
-channel discontinuities in the Regge limit

Next we have to analyze the discontinuities on the right hand side of the optical theorem (
103
). The discontinuity of the scalar
s
-channel block was recently computed in general without taking the Regge limit in
Caron-Huot:2020nem
and we will review it here. The generalization to external spinning operators is done in section
4.4
, after taking the Regge limit. Let us take the conformal partial wave expansion (
29
) in the
s
channel and use the symmetry of the integrand to extend the integration region at the cost of a factor
1/2

\displaystyle A^{1234}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{J}\int_{\frac{d}{2}-i\infty}^{\frac{d}{2}+i\infty}\frac{d\Delta}{2\pi i}\,I^{1234}(\Delta,J)\,\psi^{1234}_{\text{good},\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})\,.

(135)

Let

\psi^{1234}(z,\overline{z})
be the partial wave
\Psi^{1234}(y_{i})
with the prefactor
T^{1234}
stripped off.
\psi^{1234}_{\text{good},\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})
is the conformal partial wave with an additional term that
vanishes for integer spin but ensures favorable properties for non-integer spin
Caron-Huot:2020nem
. The new partial wave is given by

\displaystyle\psi^{1234}_{\text{good},\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})=\psi^{1234}_{\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})+2\pi\,S(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}])\,K_{J+d-1,1-\Delta}\,\xi_{\Delta,J}^{(a,b)}\,g^{1234}_{J+d-1,1-\Delta}(z,\overline{z})\,,

(136)

where
g^{1234}_{\Delta,J}(z,\overline{z})
is the usual conformal block, the constants
a,b
are defined below (
60
) and

\displaystyle\xi_{\Delta,J}^{(a,b)}=

\displaystyle\left(s^{(a,b)}_{\Delta+J}-s^{(a,b)}_{\Delta+2-d-J}\right)\frac{\Gamma\big(-J-\tfrac{d-2}{2}\big)}{\Gamma(-J)}\,,

(137)

\displaystyle s^{(a,b)}_{\beta}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sin\big(\pi(a+\beta/2)\big)\,\sin\big(\pi(b+\beta/2)\big)}{\sin(\pi\beta)}\,,

\displaystyle K_{\Delta,J}=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(\Delta-1)}{\Gamma\big(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\big)}\,\kappa^{(a,b)}_{\Delta+J}\,,

\displaystyle\kappa^{(a,b)}_{\beta}=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{\beta}{2}-a\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{\beta}{2}+a\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{\beta}{2}-b\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{\beta}{2}+b\big)}{2\pi^{2}\Gamma(\beta-1)\Gamma(\beta)}\,.

With this conformal partial wave it is possible to compute the discontinuity exactly
Caron-Huot:2020nem

\frac{\Disc_{14}\,\psi^{1234}_{\text{good},\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})}{S(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}])}=\frac{R^{1234}_{\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})}{\pi i\kappa_{\Delta,J}^{(a,b)}}\,.

(138)

Here
R
is the so-called Regge block

\displaystyle R^{1234}_{\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle=g^{1234}_{1-J,1-\Delta}-\kappa^{\prime(a,b)}_{\Delta+J}g^{1234}_{\Delta,J}-\frac{\Gamma(d-\Delta-1)\Gamma\big(\Delta-\tfrac{d}{2}\big)}{\Gamma(\Delta-1)\Gamma\big(\tfrac{d}{2}-\Delta\big)}\,\kappa^{\prime(a,b)}_{d-\Delta+J}\ g^{1234}_{d-\Delta,J}+

(139)

\displaystyle+\frac{\Gamma(J+d-2)\Gamma\big(-J-\tfrac{d-2}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(J+\tfrac{d-2}{2}\big)\Gamma(-J)}\,\kappa^{\prime(a,b)}_{\Delta+J}\ \kappa^{\prime(a,b)}_{d-\Delta+J}\ g^{1234}_{J+d-1,1-\Delta}\,,

with
\kappa^{\prime(a,b)}_{\beta}
defined as

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime(a,b)}_{\beta}=\frac{r^{(a,b)}_{\beta}}{r^{(a,b)}_{2-\beta}}\,,\qquad r^{(a,b)}_{\beta}=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2}+a)\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2}+b)}{\Gamma(\beta)}\,.

(140)

\Disc_{23}
in the
3412
OPE channel can be obtained by using (
104
) on (
138
)

\frac{\Disc_{23}\,\psi^{3412}_{\text{good},\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})}{S(\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}[\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger}])}=\frac{R^{3412}_{\mathcal{O}}(z,\overline{z})}{\pi i\kappa_{\Delta,J}^{(-b,-a)}}\,,

(141)

which was the reason to consider this channel for the correlators on the right hand side of (
103
).
The Regge block is dominated in the Regge limit by
Caron-Huot:2020nem
;
Caron_Huot_2017
;
Cornalba:2007fs

g^{1234}_{1-J,1-\Delta}(z,\overline{z})=\frac{4\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}\Gamma(\Delta-\frac{d}{2})}{\Gamma(\Delta-1)}\sigma^{1-J}\left(\Omega_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(\rho)+O(\sigma)\right)\,.

(142)

The
\sigma,\rho
cross-ratios introduced here are defined as

\sigma=\sqrt{z\overline{z}}=\sqrt{x^{2}\overline{x}^{2}}\,,\quad\cosh(\rho)=\frac{z+\overline{z}}{2\sqrt{z\overline{z}}}=-\frac{x\cdot\overline{x}}{\sqrt{x^{2}\overline{x}^{2}}}\,.

(143)

\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)
is the harmonic function on
d-1
dimensional hyperbolic space
H_{d-1}
transverse to the scattering plane in
\text{AdS}_{d+1}

Cornalba:2006xk

\displaystyle\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)={}

\displaystyle-\frac{i\nu\sin(\pi i\nu)\Gamma(h-1+i\nu)\Gamma(h-1-i\nu)}{2^{2h-1}\pi^{h+\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\big(h-\frac{1}{2}\big)}

(144)

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\left(h-1+i\nu,h-1-i\nu,h-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1-\cosh(\rho)}{2}\right)\,.

Inserting everything into (
135
), we find the following expression for the discontinuity of the correlator in the Regge limit

\Disc_{14}A^{1234}(z,\overline{z})=2\pi i\sum\limits_{J}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\ \alpha(\nu,J)\,\sigma^{1-J}\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)\,,

(145)

with

\alpha(\nu,J)=-\,\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-2}\,S\big(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\big[\big(-i\nu-\frac{d}{2}\big)^{\dagger}\big]\big)\,\Gamma(i\nu)}{2\pi\kappa_{i\nu+\frac{d}{2},J}^{(a,b)}\,\Gamma\big(i\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1\big)}\,I^{1234}\!\left(i\nu+\frac{d}{2},J\right).

(146)

As in flat space the sum in (
145
) is dominated by the large
J
contributions in the Regge limit and only finite due to a conspiration of the coefficients to ensure Regge boundedness.
The next step is therefore to perform a Sommerfeld-Watson resummation over
J
to evaluate (
145
). Also, note that the spectral function, as given by the Lorentzian inversion formula
Caron_Huot_2017
, is of the form

\displaystyle I^{1234}(\nu)=I^{1234,t}(\nu)+(-1)^{J}I^{1234,u}(\nu)\,.

(147)

Let us first consider the case of a correlator with pairwise equal external operators i.e.
a=b=0
,
I^{1234,t}=I^{1234,u}
, where only even spins are exchanged.
Now for the resummation we replace the sum by an integral,

2\sum\limits_{J\text{ even}}\to\int_{C}dJ\frac{e^{i\pi J}}{1-e^{i\pi J}}\,.

(148)

The contour
C
encloses all poles on the positive real axis (at even integers) in a clockwise direction.
The leading Regge trajectory is given by the operators with the lowest dimension
\Delta(J)
for every even spin
J
and
\alpha(\nu,J)
has poles at
i\nu=\pm(\Delta(J)-d/2)
.
Defining the inverse function
j=j(\nu)
of the spectral function
\Delta(J)
by

\nu^{2}+\big(\Delta(j(\nu))-\tfrac{d}{2}\big)^{2}=0\,,

(149)

we see that the poles in
\nu
translate into a single pole at
J=j(\nu)
.
By deforming the
J
contour to the left one sees that the
J
integral is given by the residue at
J=j(\nu)
, i.e.

\Disc_{14}A^{1234}(z,\overline{z})=2\pi i\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\ \alpha(\nu)\,\sigma^{1-j(\nu)}\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)\,,

(150)

where

\alpha(\nu)=-\underset{J=j(\nu)}{\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}}i\,\frac{e^{i\pi J}}{1-e^{-i\pi J}}\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-2}\,S\big(\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\big[\big(-i\nu-\frac{d}{2}\big)^{\dagger}\big]\big)\,\Gamma(i\nu)}{\kappa_{i\nu+\frac{d}{2},J}^{(a,b)}\,\Gamma\!\big(i\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1\big)}\,I^{1234,t}\left(i\nu+\frac{d}{2},J\right).

(151)

When the operators are not pairwise equal, the even and odd spin operators organize into two analytic families as evident from the Lorentzian inversion formula
Caron_Huot_2017
. To obtain the contribution of the leading Regge trajectory we still sum over the even spin exchanges. The result is of the same form as in (
150
) with
I^{1234,t}
replaced by
\frac{1}{2}I^{1234}
in
\alpha(\nu)
.
For the other correlator we can use (
104
) to see that we get an analogous result with the complex conjugate spectral function

\Disc_{23}A^{3412}(z,\overline{z})=2\pi i\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\ \alpha(\nu)^{*}\,\sigma^{1-j(\nu)}\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)\,.

(152)

One can show that the corresponding impact parameter representation is given in general
by the same spectral function times a multiplicative factor which cancels poles for the external double-trace operators
Cornalba:2006xm

\mathcal{B}(p,{\overline{p}})=2\pi i\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\ \beta(\nu)\,S^{j(\nu)-1}\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,,

(153)

where

\beta(\nu)=\frac{4\pi^{2-d}(\sqrt{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{3}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{O}_{4}}})^{-1}\;\alpha(\nu)}{\chi_{j(\nu)}(\nu)\,\chi_{j(\nu)}(-\nu)}\,,

(154)

with the definition

\chi_{j(\nu)}(\nu)=\Gamma\left(\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+j(\nu)-d/2+i\nu}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}+j(\nu)-d/2+i\nu}{2}\right).

(155)

The impact parameter space cross-ratios, analogous to (
143
), are

S=\sqrt{p^{2}{\overline{p}}^{2}},\qquad\cosh L=-\frac{p\cdot\overline{p}}{\sqrt{p^{2}{\overline{p}}^{2}}}\,.

(156)

In the dual AdS scattering process these cross ratios are interpreted as the squared of the energy with respect to global time and as
the impact parameter in the transverse space
H_{d-1}
.

4.4
Spinning particles and the vertex function

In this section we will introduce concrete expressions for the tree amplitudes with spinning external legs and show that the contributions of the contracted spinning legs can be expressed in terms of a scalar function of three spectral parameters which we call the vertex function, analogous to (
87
) in flat space.
We construct tensor structures in terms of differential operators, which are a Regge limit version of weight-shifting operators that generate spinning conformal blocks from the scalar ones
Costa:2011dw
;
Karateev:2017jgd
.
It is convenient to work with tensor structures which are homogeneous in
p
and
\overline{p}
, i.e. independent of the cross-ratio
S
in (
156
), such that all tensor structures have the same large
S
behavior in the Regge limit. These differential operators can be constructed from the covariant derivative on the hyperboloid
H_{d-1}
and from
\widehat{p}=p/|p|,\widehat{\overline{p}}=\overline{p}/|\overline{p}|

Cornalba:2009ax
;
Costa:2017twz
.
The possible differential operators that generate spin for a single particle are

\mathcal{D}^{\rho,k}_{\mathbf{m}}(p)=\widehat{p}_{m_{1}}\ldots\widehat{p}_{m_{k}}{\nabla_{p}}_{m_{k+1}}\ldots{\nabla_{p}}_{m_{|\rho|}}\,,\qquad k=0,\ldots,|\rho|\,.

(157)

Tree diagrams for exchange of the Pomeron then have the form

\mathcal{B}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(p,\overline{p})=2\pi i\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\,S^{j(\nu)-1}\mathfrak{D}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,.

(158)

Here

\mathcal{B}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(p,\overline{p})
is defined just as in (
125
) and (
133
), but with tensor structures constructed from
\widehat{p}
and
\widehat{\overline{p}}
.
In (
158
) we introduced the following definition for the combination of spectral functions
\beta(\nu)
and differential operators that generate different tensor structures

\mathfrak{D}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(\nu)=\sum\limits_{k_{5}=0}^{|\rho_{5}|}\sum\limits_{k_{6}=0}^{|\rho_{6}|}\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)\mathcal{D}^{\rho_{5},k_{5}}_{\mathbf{m}}(p)\mathcal{D}^{\rho_{6},k_{6}}_{\mathbf{n}}(\overline{p})\,.

(159)

Notice that, in contrast to flat space, we do not impose a full factorization into three-point structures but rather allow for a separate spectral function for each combination of three-point structures.

The next step is to derive the general functional form of (
134
) after the contractions and sums have been done. We begin by inserting (
158
) into (
134
),

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(p,{\overline{p}})={}

\displaystyle 2\pi^{2}\sum\limits_{\Delta_{5},\Delta_{6},\rho_{5},\rho_{6}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}\,S^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}

(160)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(\nu_{1})^{*}\,\Omega_{i\nu_{1}}(L)\,\pi_{\rho_{5}}^{\mathbf{m};\mathbf{p}}\pi_{\rho_{6}}^{\mathbf{n};\mathbf{q}}\,\mathfrak{D}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q}}(\nu_{2})\,\Omega_{i\nu_{2}}(L)\,.

Here
\pi_{\rho}
is the projector to the irreducible representation
\rho
of
SO(d)
, which is necessary because the operators (
157
) do not ensure the properties of irreducible representations such as tracelessness and Young symmetrization.
Next we will show how one can replace the contractions and derivatives in the previous equation by spectral parameters.
Note first that due to
p\cdot\nabla_{p}=0
, all contractions involving
\widehat{p}
or
\widehat{{\overline{p}}}
give factors of their norm
-1
. The remaining contractions involve only covariant derivatives. These contracted derivatives can all be replaced by functions of the spectral parameters by using the Laplace equation for the harmonic function

\left(\nabla_{H_{d-1}}^{2}+\nu^{2}+(d/2-1)^{2}\right)\Omega_{i\nu}(L)=0\,.

(161)

Using this equation, factors of
\nabla_{p}^{2}
can directly be replaced.
To evaluate contractions between derivatives acting on different harmonic functions we expand the product of two scalar harmonic functions as follows,

\Omega_{i\nu_{1}}(L)\Omega_{i\nu_{2}}(L)=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\,\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,,

(162)

where
\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)
was computed (for the similar case of harmonic functions on AdS
d+1
) in appendix D of
Penedones:2010ue
.
7
7

7

Note that
\Phi_{\text{here}}\Omega_{i\nu}(0)=\Phi_{\text{there}}
.

By acting repeatedly with (
161
) on this equation, one can determine the function
W_{k}
that appears in

{\nabla_{p}}_{m_{1}}\ldots{\nabla_{p}}_{m_{k}}\Omega_{i\nu_{1}}(L)\nabla_{p}^{m_{1}}\ldots\nabla_{p}^{m_{k}}\Omega_{i\nu_{2}}(L)=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\,W_{k}\big(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2}\big)\,\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,.

(163)

W_{k}
is a fixed kinematical polynomial of maximal degree
k
in its arguments.
For example, the first non-trivial case is

\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!d\nu\,\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\nu^{2}\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)=\left(\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}+(\tfrac{d}{2}-1)^{2}\right)\Omega_{i\nu_{1}}(L)\,\Omega_{i\nu_{2}}(L)-2\nabla_{\mu}\Omega_{i\nu_{1}}(L)\nabla^{\mu}\Omega_{i\nu_{2}}(L),

(164)

from which one can read off
W_{0}
and
W_{1}
to be

W_{0}\big(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2}\big)=1\,,\qquad W_{1}\big(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}-\nu^{2}+(d/2-1)^{2}\Big).

(165)

More generally, by acting with the Laplacian on both sides of (
163
) one can derive a recursion relation of the form

\displaystyle\int d\nu\,W_{k+1}(\nu_{i})\,\Phi(\nu_{i})\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)=\int d\nu\,W_{k}(\nu_{i})\,W_{1}(\nu_{i})\,\Phi(\nu_{i})\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)

(166)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left([\nabla^{2},\nabla_{m_{1}}\dots\nabla_{m_{k}}]\Omega_{i\nu_{1}}(L)\nabla^{m_{1}}\dots\nabla^{m_{k}}\Omega_{i\nu_{2}}(L)+(\nu_{1}\leftrightarrow\nu_{2})\right)\,.

The terms with commutators, which will vanish in the flat space limit, can be evaluated using the fact that the commutators of covariant derivatives can be replaced by Riemann tensors, which for the hyperboloid can be written in terms of the metric. This means that these terms have two derivatives less than the other terms, and will therefore produce less than maximal powers of
\nu_{i}
. This shows that the maximal power of
\nu_{i}
in
W_{k}
is just given by repeatedly multiplying
W_{1}
. Therefore we have

W_{k}\big(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2}\big)=\left(\frac{\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}-\nu^{2}}{2}\right)^{k}+O\!\left(\nu_{i}^{2(k-1)}\right).

(167)

Having shown that all derivatives can be replaced by polynomials of the spectral parameters, we can define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(\nu_{1})^{*}\,\Omega_{i\nu_{1}}(L)\,\pi_{\rho_{5}}^{\mathbf{m};\mathbf{p}}\pi_{\rho_{6}}^{\mathbf{n};\mathbf{q}}\,\mathfrak{D}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q}}(\nu_{2})\,\Omega_{i\nu_{2}}(L)

(168)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\,W_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}\big(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2}\big)\,\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,.

This gives the contribution of a given pair of intermediate states labeled by
(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5})
and
(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})
to
-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(p,{\overline{p}})
.
Now we can define the vertex function
V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)
, which is even in all its arguments, in analogy to (
87
) as the sum over all such contributions in (
160
)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\Delta_{5},\Delta_{6},\rho_{5},\rho_{6},}W_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}\big(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2}\big)=\beta(\nu_{1})^{*}\beta(\nu_{2})V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}\,,

(169)

and reach the following representation for the 1-loop amplitude

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(p,{\overline{p}})=2\pi^{2}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}\,\beta(\nu_{1})^{*}\beta(\nu_{2})\,V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}

(170)

\displaystyle S^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,.

All the information about the spinning tree-level correlators and their contractions is encoded in the vertex function
V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)
which mirrors the role of its flat space analogue.

However, in order to compute the full impact parameter representation rather than just its real part, we have to go through a detour via the Lorentzian inversion formula, as described in
Meltzer:2019pyl
.
We first Fourier transform back to
\text{dDisc}_{t}\,A_{\text{1-loop}}
from which we obtain the
s
-channel OPE coefficients.
Then we can compute
\Disc_{14}A_{\text{1-loop}}
which we can finally Fourier transform to obtain
\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(p,{\overline{p}})
. Since in the Regge limit the difference between
\text{dDisc}_{t}\,A_{\text{1-loop}}
and
\Disc_{14}A_{\text{1-loop}}
is just a phase factor (see
Meltzer:2019pyl
), the same happens for the impact parameter representation

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(p,{\overline{p}})=-4\pi^{2}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}\,

\displaystyle\frac{1+e^{-i\pi(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1)}}{1-e^{-2\pi i(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1)}}\,\beta(\nu_{1})^{*}\beta(\nu_{2})\,V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}

\displaystyle S^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}\;\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,.

(171)

It is important to emphasize that this provides a finite
\Delta_{gap}
description for the one-loop correlator in the Regge limit up to the knowledge of the vertex function
V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}
. For CFTs that admit a flat space limit,
we will see in sections
6
and
7
how one can fix part of this vertex function from the knowledge of its flat space analogue. In section
5
below, we make a comparison with the large
\Delta_{gap}
limit studied in reference
Meltzer:2019pyl
, and also describe the implications of (
170
) for t-channel CFT data.

5
Constraints on CFT data

5.1
Comparison with the large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
limit

In
Meltzer:2019pyl
the Regge limit of the four-point correlator of pairwise identical scalars was studied in an expansion in
1/N
in the limit of large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
. The specific limit considered was
S\gg\Delta_{\text{gap}}^{2}\gg 1
, so the result is sensitive to all the higher spin interactions in the leading Regge trajectory, but tidal excitations are ignored. Since we have kept
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
finite, we should be able to obtain a match between the result for the one-loop correlator in
Meltzer:2019pyl
with our result (
134
) after dropping the tidal excitations
\mathcal{O}_{5}\neq\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{6}\neq\mathcal{O}_{3}
.

We pick the term
\Delta_{5}=\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{6}=\Delta_{3}
that is the sole contribution to (
134
) in the large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
limit, and use (
153
) to obtain

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(S,L)=2\pi^{2}\int d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}d\nu\,\beta^{*}(\nu_{1})\beta(\nu_{2})\,\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,S^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,\Bigg|_{\left[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\right]}\,.

(172)

Now let us extract the corresponding result from
Meltzer:2019pyl
. Equation (3.15) of
Meltzer:2019pyl

gives the double discontinuity of the one-loop correlator
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
as follows (with
\Delta_{\phi}=\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{\psi}=\Delta_{3}
)

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{t}[\mathcal{G}^{(2)}(z,\overline{z})]=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{4}}{8}\int d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}d\nu\,\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(\nu)\,\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(-\nu)\,\mathcal{N}

(173)

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\,\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)(z\overline{z})^{\frac{2-j(\nu_{1})-j(\nu_{2})}{2}}\Omega_{i\nu}\left(\frac{1}{2}\log(z/\overline{z})\right)\,,

where
\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(\nu)
is defined in (
155
) but with
j(\nu)
replaced by
j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1
, and with
\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{4}=\Delta_{3}
. It accounts for the double-trace exchanges
[\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{1}]
and
[\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{3}]
analytically continued to spin
j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1
. The operators contributing to the
t
-channel expansion in the large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
limit are the double-traces
[\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}]_{n,\ell}
with dimensions and OPE coefficients given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{h,\overline{h}}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{(0)}_{h,\overline{h}}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\,\gamma^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}+\frac{1}{N^{4}}\,\gamma^{(2)}_{h,\overline{h}}+\cdots\,,\qquad\Delta^{(0)}_{h,\overline{h}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}+2n+\ell\,,

(174)

\displaystyle P_{h,\overline{h}}

\displaystyle=P^{MFT}_{h,\overline{h}}\left(1+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\,\delta P^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}+\frac{1}{N^{4}}\,\delta P^{(2)}_{h,\overline{h}}+\cdots\right)\,,

where
h,\overline{h}=\Delta\mp\ell
.
The tree-level anomalous dimensions
\gamma^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}
and tree-level corrections to OPE coefficients
\delta P^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}
can be extracted respectively from
\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu)
and
\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu)
by

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}

\displaystyle\approx\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu)\,(h\overline{h})^{j(\nu)-1}\,\Omega_{i\nu}\big(\log(h/\overline{h})\big)\,,

(175)

\displaystyle\delta P^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}

\displaystyle\approx\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu)\,(h\overline{h})^{j(\nu)-1}\,\Omega_{i\nu}\big(\log(h/\overline{h})\big)\,.

\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu)
and
\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu)
can be obtained respectively from the real and imaginary parts of the phase shift, and are related to
\beta
by
8
8

8

Note that due to difference in conventions,
\mathcal{N}\beta
for us is equal to
\beta
, as defined in
Meltzer:2019pyl
.

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu)=

\displaystyle 2\,\text{Re}\beta(\nu)\,,

(176)

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu)=

\displaystyle-2\pi\,\text{Im}\beta(\nu)\,.

Taking the Fourier transform to impact parameter space on (
173
) and then using (
176
) gives
9
9

9

In our conventions the Fourier transform takes
\text{dDisc}_{t}[\mathcal{G}^{(2)}(z,\overline{z})]
to
-\mathcal{N}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}
upto scaling factors.

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(S,L)=2\pi^{2}\int d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}d\nu\,\text{Re}\beta(\nu_{1})\,\text{Re}\beta(\nu_{2})\,\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)S^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,.

(177)

We need to compare (
172
) with (
177
). The only difference are the real parts in (
177
), however
\text{Im}\beta
in (
172
) is related to tree-level corrections to the OPE coefficients and these are suppressed at large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}

Meltzer:2019pyl
. This can be seen for example from (
176
) and using in it the explicit expression for
\alpha(\nu)
from
Meltzer:2019pyl
. The result is

\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu)=\frac{-\pi\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left(\frac{ie^{i\pi j(\nu)}}{1-e^{i\pi j(\nu)}}\right)}{\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\left(\frac{ie^{i\pi j(\nu)}}{1-e^{i\pi j(\nu)}}\right)}\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu)=-\pi\tan\Big(\frac{\pi}{2}j(\nu)\Big)\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu)\,.

(178)

The suppression is due to the tan factor, since for large
N
theories it is known that
Brower:2006ea
;
Cornalba:2007fs
;
Costa:2012cb

\displaystyle j(\nu)=2-2\,\frac{\nu^{2}+(d/2)^{2}}{\Delta_{\text{gap}}^{2}}+O\big(\Delta_{\text{gap}}^{-4}\big)\,.

(179)

The anomalous dimensions
\gamma^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}
are order 1, while the the corrections to the OPE coefficients
\delta P^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}
are at order
\Delta_{\text{gap}}^{-2}
.

Thus we have matched our result for the Regge limit of the dDisc of a one-loop correlator at large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
to that of
Meltzer:2019pyl
. Note that we managed to reproduce the result without the need for any projections to the physical double-traces. Therefore it is reasonable to assume that the gluing of tree-level correlators in the Regge limit does not receive contributions from the double-traces of shadows and we can use the optical theorem (
134
) without the projections onto
[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}]
.

5.2
Extracting t-channel CFT data

Next we shall see how we can extract the CFT data for the double-trace operators exchanged in the
t
-channel to order
1/N^{4}
from the vertex function
V(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2})
. To this end we follow section 3.2 of
Meltzer:2019pyl
and extend the results therein by including tidal excitations, which make our statements valid at finite
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
.
As discussed in the previous section, the only operators contributing to the
t
-channel expansion in the large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
limit are the double-traces
[\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}]

Li:2017lmh
. The three-point function of these double-traces with their constituent operators
\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{3}

has the large
N
behavior

\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}[\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}]\rangle\sim 1\,.

(180)

By including tidal excitations we have to include also double-traces
[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}]
corresponding to additional double-traces coupling to

\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{3}
. These satisfy

\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}]\rangle\sim\frac{1}{N^{2}}\,,\qquad[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}]\neq[\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}]\,,

(181)

so that only their classical dimension and leading OPE coefficient squared
10
10

10

We are free to insert
P^{\text{MFT}}
here, defining
\delta P
accordingly. This will be useful below in (
189
).

\Delta_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{O}_{5}}+\Delta_{\mathcal{O}_{6}}+2n+\ell\,,\qquad P_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{N^{4}}P^{\text{MFT}}_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}\delta P^{(56)}_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}+\ldots\,,

(182)

appear in the one-loop correlator. This is compatible with the large
N
behavior for single-trace exchange in the direct channel,

\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta(J)}\rangle\sim\frac{1}{N}~,~\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta(J)}\mathcal{O}_{6}\mathcal{O}_{3}\rangle\sim\frac{1}{N}\,,

(183)

which justifies that the OPE coefficients
c_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}]}
start at order
1/N^{2}
.
As explained in
Meltzer:2019pyl
, the cross channel expansion of the correlator is then dominated by the terms

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\text{1-loop}}^{\circlearrowleft}(z,\overline{z})}{(z\overline{z})^{\Delta_{\phi}}}

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{h,\overline{h}}P^{MFT}_{h,\overline{h}}\bigg[i\pi\gamma^{(2)}_{h,\overline{h}}+\delta P^{(2)}_{h,\overline{h}}+i\pi\gamma^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}\,\delta P^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}\left(\gamma^{(1)}_{h,\overline{h}}\right)^{2}\bigg]g_{h,\overline{h}}(1-z,1-\overline{z})

\displaystyle+\sum_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}P^{MFT}_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}\delta P^{(56)}_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}\,g_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}(1-z,1-\overline{z})\,.

(184)

We shall now compare with our result for the one-loop correlator in the Regge limit and use it in the light of (
184
) to extract CFT data. We start with the dDisc of the correlator in the impact parameter representation as in (
170
). Doing an inverse Fourier transform on this and taking out the appropriate scale factors gives us the dDisc of the one-loop correlator in the Regge limit

\displaystyle\text{dDisc}_{t}\mathcal{A}_{\text{1-loop}}(z,\overline{z})=\frac{\pi^{4}\mathcal{N}}{4}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}\,\big(\beta^{*}(\nu_{1})\beta(\nu_{2})+\beta(\nu_{1})\beta^{*}(\nu_{2})\big)V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}

(185)

\displaystyle\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(\nu)\,\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(-\nu)\,\sigma^{2-j(\nu_{1})-j(\nu_{2})}\,\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)\,,

where we symmetrized the product of
\beta
’s by using the symmetry of the expression under
\nu_{1}\leftrightarrow\nu_{2}
.
We can now use the Lorentzian inversion formula
Caron_Huot_2017
on (
185
), as shown in
Meltzer:2019pyl
, to obtain the one-loop correlator in the Regge limit, and then use (
176
) to express it as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\text{1-loop}}^{\circlearrowleft}(z,\overline{z})\approx-\frac{\pi^{4}\mathcal{N}}{4}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}d\nu\,\frac{1+e^{-i\pi(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1)}}{1-e^{-2\pi i(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1)}}\,V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}

(186)

\displaystyle\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(\nu)\,\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(-\nu)\,\sigma^{2-j(\nu_{1})-j(\nu_{2})}\,\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})+\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\right].

We now take the
t
-channel expansion (
184
), and use in it (
175
), (
162
), and the following ansatz,

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{h,\overline{h}}

\displaystyle\approx\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}d\nu\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,(h\overline{h})^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}\,\Omega_{i\nu}\big(\log(h/\overline{h})\big)\,,

(187)

\displaystyle\delta P^{(2)/(56)}_{h,\overline{h}}

\displaystyle\approx\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}d\nu\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(2)/(56)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,(h\overline{h})^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}\,\Omega_{i\nu}\big(\log(h/\overline{h})\big)\,,

to obtain

\displaystyle(z\overline{z})^{-\Delta_{\phi}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\text{1-loop}}^{\circlearrowleft}(z,\overline{z})\approx\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}d\nu\Bigg[\sum_{h,\overline{h}}(h\overline{h})^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}\,\Omega_{i\nu}(\log h/\overline{h})\,P^{MFT}_{h,\overline{h}}

(188)

\displaystyle\bigg[i\pi\widehat{\gamma}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)+\frac{i\pi}{2}\left(\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})+\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\right)\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)+\widehat{\delta P}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\bigg]\,g_{h,\overline{h}}(1-z,1-\overline{z})

\displaystyle+\sum_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}P^{MFT}_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(56)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)(h^{\prime}\overline{h}^{\prime})^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2)}\,\Omega_{i\nu}(\log h^{\prime}/\overline{h}^{\prime})\,g_{h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}}(1-z,1-\overline{z})\Bigg]\,.

We can approximate the
h,\overline{h}
and
h^{\prime},\overline{h}^{\prime}
sums with integrals,
\sum_{h,\overline{h}}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}dh\,d\overline{h}
, and evaluate them using Bessel function integrals (see section 2.2 of
Meltzer:2019pyl
) to arrive at the result

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\text{1-loop}}^{\circlearrowleft}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\pi^{2}\mathcal{N}}{4}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}d\nu\,\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(\nu)\,\chi_{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1}(-\nu)\,\sigma^{2-j(\nu_{1})-j(\nu_{2})}\,\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)

\displaystyle\left[i\pi\widehat{\gamma}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\,\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)+\widehat{\delta P}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\right.

(189)

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{i\pi}{2}\left(\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})+\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\right)\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)+\widehat{\delta P}^{(56)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\right].

Comparing the real parts of the coefficient of
\chi(\nu)\chi(-\nu)\sigma^{2-j(\nu_{1})-j(\nu_{2})}\Omega_{i\nu}(\rho)
in the integrands of (
186
) and (
189
), and using

\frac{1+e^{-i\pi(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1)}}{1-e^{-2\pi i(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-1)}}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\big(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})\big)\right),

(190)

we conclude that

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta P}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2};\nu)+\widehat{\delta P}^{(56)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2};\nu)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\pi^{2}\Big(V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}-1\Big)\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})

(191)

\displaystyle+V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\bigg]\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,.

This is the general result for fixed
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
that extracts OPE data from the AdS vertex function.
Let us now take the large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
limit to make contact with
Meltzer:2019pyl
.
In this limit,
V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}=1
and
\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}
,
\widehat{\delta P}^{(56)}
are suppressed with respect to

\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}
. Therefore
\delta{P}^{(2)}_{h,\overline{h}}=0
, as was obtained in
Meltzer:2019pyl
.

Similarly, comparing the imaginary parts
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2};\nu)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\left(\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})+\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\right)+\pi\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{2}(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2}))\right)

\displaystyle\!\!V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}\Big(\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})+\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\Big)\bigg]\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,.

(192)

The term
\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\,\widehat{\delta P}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})
is suppressed by
\Delta_{\text{gap}}^{-4}
with respect to
the other terms. At leading order in
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
, we can discard this term and set
V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}=1
. We can then use (
178
) to simplify the expression to,

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2};\nu)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\pi\tan\left(\frac{1}{2}\pi j(\nu_{1})\right)\tan\left(\frac{1}{2}\pi j(\nu_{2})\right)\tan\left(\frac{1}{2}\pi(j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2}))\right)

(193)

\displaystyle\times\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{1})\widehat{\gamma}^{(1)}(\nu_{2})\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,.

This is the same result as obtained in
Meltzer:2019pyl
for
\widehat{\gamma}^{(2)}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2};\nu)
.
More generally, knowledge of the vertex function
V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}
and of the
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}[\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}]\rangle
OPE coefficients gives additional information about the one-loop CFT data of the
[\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{3}]
double-trace operators. It would be interesting to
analyze these equations order by order in the
1/\Delta_{\text{gap}}^{2}
expansion.

6
Flat space limit

Having fixed the general form of the impact parameter representation of the one-loop correlator from first principles in section
4
,
we now want to fix part of the dynamical data by taking the flat space limit,
which relates it to the known flat space amplitudes.
The prescription to achieve this was discovered in

Cornalba:2007fs
, where it was applied to scalar tree-level amplitudes.
This limit is taken by sending the AdS radius
R
to infinity while scaling the relevant quantities in order to match them to flat space quantities in a sensible way.
The dimensionless quantities
S
and
L
are sent to dimensionless combinations of
R
with the flat space center of mass energy
s
and impact parameter
b
as

S=\frac{R^{2}s}{4}\,,\qquad L=\frac{b}{R}\,.

(194)

Note that
L
is the AdS impact parameter, as it describes the geodesic distance on
H_{d-1}
between the impact points in transverse space.
If we impose the identification of Casimir eigenvalues

\Delta(\Delta-d)=R^{2}m^{2}\,,

(195)

for the states on the leading Regge trajectory and take this equation off-shell,
it becomes

\nu^{2}+\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{2}=R^{2}q^{2}\,,

(196)

so for large
R
we further impose

\nu^{2}=R^{2}q^{2}\,.

(197)

Our expressions in AdS are integrals in
\nu
, while in flat space we have vector integrals in
q
,
where we recall that
q
is a vector in the transverse space
\mathbb{R}^{D-2}
.
In order to compare the expressions, it is instructive to do the flat space angular integrals and keep only the integral over the modulus
|q|
.
In this way, the exponential is replaced by the harmonic function
\omega_{q}(b)
according to

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{D-2}}\frac{dq}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}\,e^{ib\cdot q}=2\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d|q|\,\omega_{q}(b)\,,

(198)

so that
Costa:2014kfa

\displaystyle\omega_{q}(b)=q\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{D-2}}\frac{dp}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}\,e^{ib\cdot p}\,\delta(p^{2}-q^{2})=\frac{1}{2(2\pi)^{\frac{D-2}{2}}}\frac{|q|^{\frac{D-2}{2}}}{|b|^{\frac{D-4}{2}}}J_{\frac{D-4}{2}}(|q||b|)\,,

(199)

where
J
denotes the Bessel
J
-function and we recall that
\omega_{q}(b)
only depends on the modulus of the vectors
q
and
b
.
One can check that the flat space limit of the
H_{d-1}
harmonic function (
144
) yields the flat space harmonic function

R^{3-D}\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\to\omega_{q}(b)\,,\qquad\nu\geq 0\,.

(200)

For even
d
this can be checked directly, while for general
d
it is convenient to use an integral representation for the hypergeometric function which under the limit is related to an integral representation for the Bessel function
Carmi:2018qzm
.
For even
d
the relation is also valid for
\nu<0
.

In the context of string theory we further have the dimensionless coupling
\lambda
which is expressed in terms of
\alpha^{\prime}
and
R
as

\sqrt{\lambda}=\frac{R^{2}}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,,

(201)

meaning we can also express
S
as

S=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}\,\alpha^{\prime}s}{4}\,.

(202)

To summarize, the flat space limit is taken by
sending the AdS radius
R
to infinity while replacing

S=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}\alpha^{\prime}s}{4},\quad L=\frac{b}{R},\quad\nu^{2}=R^{2}q^{2},\quad\nu_{1}^{2}=R^{2}q_{1}^{2},\quad\nu_{2}^{2}=R^{2}q_{2}^{2},\quad\sqrt{\lambda}=\frac{R^{2}}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,,

(203)

and impact parameter representations can be compared by using (
200
).
We can also use these relations to relate
\Delta_{gap}
to
\lambda
taking as reference a string state of mass
m^{2}=4/\alpha^{\prime}
, therefore

\Delta^{2}_{\text{gap}}=\frac{4R^{2}}{\alpha^{\prime}}=4\sqrt{\lambda}\,.

(204)

6.1
Matching in impact parameter space

Let us now see what we can learn when we apply the flat space limit to the impact parameter representations studied in section
4
. We begin with the tree-level correlator of four scalars for which the limit was originally imposed in
Cornalba:2007fs
.
The flat space limit of the AdS result
\mathcal{B}
in (
153
) should match the flat space impact parameter representation
i\delta
from (
85
) of the amplitude (
78
)
11
11

11

The relative factor
i
in
\mathcal{B}\to i\delta
can be determined by matching the exponents in the eikonal approximation for
\lambda\to\infty
.

\mathcal{B}_{\text{tree}}(p,{\overline{p}})=4\pi i\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\,\beta(\nu)\,S^{j(\nu)-1}\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\ \to\ i\delta_{\text{tree}}(s,b)=2i\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d|q|\,\beta(t)\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}s}{4}\right)^{j(t)-1}\omega_{q}(b)\,.

(205)

Here and below we do not always write the overall factors of
R
as in (
200
), but they do work out correctly when including the expansion parameters from
(
3
) and (
71
) and using the relation

\frac{1}{N^{2}}=\frac{1}{R^{D-2}}\frac{2G_{N}}{\pi}\,.

(206)

From (
200
) and (
202
) we see that
this does indeed match, provided the flat space limit of the AdS Regge trajectory and spectral function are sent to the flat space Regge trajectory and Pomeron propagator
12
12

12

j(\nu)
,
j(t)
and
\beta(\nu)
,
\beta(t)
are different functions and not the same function with different arguments.

j(\nu)\,\to\,j(t)\,,\qquad\lambda^{\frac{j(\nu)-1}{2}}\beta(\nu)\,\to\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\beta(t)\,.

(207)

The power of
\lambda
in the relation of
\beta
’s is necessary to cancel the powers of
\lambda
in the relation between
S
and
\alpha^{\prime}s
.
It is compatible with the expectation that each derivative in the couplings of the spin
J
operators forming the Pomeron comes at least with a power of
\lambda^{-\frac{1}{4}}
.

Next we consider the optical theorem in AdS (
134
) and flat space (
86
)

-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}\\
\Delta_{6},\rho_{6}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{B}^{3652\,*}_{\text{tree}}\mathcal{B}^{1564}_{\text{tree}}\ \to\ \mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\delta_{\text{1-loop}}(b)=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{5},\rho_{5},\epsilon_{5}\\
m_{6},\rho_{6},\epsilon_{6}\end{subarray}}\delta_{\text{tree}}^{3652\,*}\delta_{\text{tree}}^{1564}\,.

(208)

The similarity is striking, however we have to make sure the sums and summands are in fact related by the flat space limit.
The additional sums over polarizations can be evaluated using completeness relations such as
(
92
), which evaluate to contractions just as in the AdS equation.
We also have to make sure that the labels
\rho
on both sides are irreducible representations of the same group
SO(d)
. This is indeed the case for massive particles if we consider the flat space limit
AdS_{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{1,d}
, which has the massive Little group
SO(d)
.

The next step is to match the tree-level correlators (
158
) and amplitudes (
78
) that involve spinning particles 5 and 6. In this case the flat space limit gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(p,\overline{p})=4\pi i\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d\nu\,S^{j(\nu)-1}\,\mathfrak{D}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\ \to

(209)

\displaystyle\to\

\displaystyle i\delta^{(m_{5},\rho_{5}),(m_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(s,b)=i\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{D-2}}\frac{dq}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}s}{4}\right)^{j(t)-1}A^{12P}_{m_{5},\rho_{5},\mathbf{m}}(q,v)\,\beta(t)A^{34P}_{m_{6},\rho_{6},\mathbf{n}}(q,v)\,e^{iq\cdot b}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=2i\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d|q|\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}s}{4}\right)^{j(t)-1}A^{12P}_{m_{5},\rho_{5},\mathbf{m}}(-i\partial_{b},v)\,\beta(t)A^{34P}_{m_{6},\rho_{6},\mathbf{n}}(-i\partial_{b},v)\,\omega_{q}(b)\,,

where the derivative
\partial_{b}
is with respect to the components of the transverse vector
b
.
The difference compared to (
205
) is that in AdS we have differential operators that generate tensor structures, while in flat space the tensor structures are the ones of the on-shell three-point amplitudes. As discussed in section
3.4
, these three-point amplitudes are given in terms of the Pomeron momentum
q
and, for massive particles, the longitudinal polarization vector
v
, which is transverse to
q
.
We will now study the relation of these two kinds of tensor structures to the flat space limit.

We begin with the covariant derivatives and will argue that they become derivatives in impact parameter in flat space, i.e.

\nabla_{p}^{m}\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\ \to\ R\partial_{b}^{m}e^{ib\cdot q}=Riq^{m}e^{ib\cdot q}\,.

(210)

In order to show this, we will act with two contracted covariant derivatives either on a single harmonic function or on two different ones, covering all situations that can occur.
Acting on a single harmonic function we obtain, from (
161
) and (
203
),

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\,\nabla_{p}^{2}\,\Omega_{i\nu_{1,2}}(L)\ \to\ -\alpha^{\prime}q_{1,2}^{2}\,\omega_{i\nu_{1,2}}(b)\,.

(211)

The action of contracted covariant derivatives on two different harmonic functions is captured by the functions
W_{k}
in (
163
), which is given in the flat space limit by the leading term (
167
)

\displaystyle\frac{W_{k}\big(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2}\big)}{\big(\sqrt{\lambda}\big)^{k}}\ \to\ \left(\frac{\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}-\nu^{2}}{2\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)^{k}\ \to\ \left(\alpha^{\prime}\,\frac{q_{1}^{2}+q_{2}^{2}-q^{2}}{2}\right)^{k}=(\alpha^{\prime})^{k}(-q_{1}\cdot q_{2})^{k}\,,

(212)

where we used that
q=q_{1}+q_{2}
. This implies that the flat space limit of (
163
) is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\big(\sqrt{\lambda}\big)^{k}}{\nabla_{p}}_{m_{1}}\ldots{\nabla_{p}}_{m_{k}}\Omega_{i\nu_{1}}(L)\nabla_{p}^{m_{1}}\ldots\nabla_{p}^{m_{k}}\Omega_{i\nu_{2}}(L)\ \to

(213)

\displaystyle\to\

\displaystyle(-\alpha^{\prime})^{k}q_{1\,m_{1}}\ldots q_{1\,m_{k}}e^{ib\cdot q_{1}}q_{2}^{m_{1}}\ldots q_{2}^{m_{k}}e^{ib\cdot q_{2}}\,.

(214)

We conclude that both (
211
) and (
214
) are compatible with (
210
).
Apart from the covariant derivative, tensor structures depend also on the direction
\widehat{p}
, which is normal to the transverse space
H_{d-1}
and satisfies
\widehat{p}^{2}=-1
. In flat space the only possible direction for polarizations that is normal to the transverse space is the unit vector
v
, hence we have to require that in the flat space limit

\widehat{p}^{m}\,\to\,iv^{m}\,.

(215)

With the identifications (
210
) and (
215
), the matching in (
209
) works provided that the spectral functions
\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)
in (
159
) are such that

\lambda^{\frac{j(\nu)-1}{2}}\mathfrak{D}^{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}_{\mathbf{m}\mathbf{n}}(\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,\to\,\frac{1}{2\pi}A^{12P}_{m_{5},\rho_{5},\mathbf{m}}(-i\partial_{b},v)\,\beta(t)\,A^{34P}_{m_{6},\rho_{6},\mathbf{n}}(-i\partial_{b},v)\,\omega_{q}(b)\,.

(216)

Using the explicit tensor structures for three-point amplitudes in (
97
), the matching (
216
) can also be expressed for the spectral function for any given tensor structure

\lambda^{\frac{j(\nu)-1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{|\rho_{5}|-k_{5}}{4}}\lambda^{\frac{|\rho_{6}|-k_{6}}{4}}\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)\ \to\ \frac{1}{2\pi}\,a_{m_{5},\rho_{5}}^{k_{5}}(t)\,a_{m_{6},\rho_{6}}^{k_{6}}(t)\,\beta(t)\,.

(217)

The powers of
\lambda
are again compatible with a factor of
\lambda^{-\frac{1}{4}}
in
\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)
for each derivative in the coupling.
Such a scaling is expected from the general arguments of
Costa:2017twz
;
Meltzer:2017rtf
.
We have now shown that all tree-level phase shifts appearing in the AdS and flat space optical theorems can be related by the flat space limit.

Let us also compare the vertex functions that appear in both AdS and flat space impact parameter representations of the one-loop amplitudes.
The flat space limit of (
170
) is given by the impact parameter transform of (
90
), i.e.

-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{\text{1-loop}}(p,{\overline{p}})\ \to\ \mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\delta_{\text{1-loop}}(s,b)\,,

(218)

becomes

\displaystyle 2\pi^{2}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu d\nu_{1}d\nu_{2}\,\beta(\nu_{1})^{*}\beta(\nu_{2})\,V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)^{2}S^{j(\nu_{1})+j(\nu_{2})-2}\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\ \to

(219)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{D-2}}\frac{dqdq_{1}dq_{2}}{(2\pi)^{2(D-2)}}\,\beta(t_{1})^{*}\beta(t_{2})\,V(q_{1},q_{2})^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}s}{4}\right)^{j(t_{1})+j(t_{2})-2}\delta(q-q_{1}-q_{2})\,e^{iq\cdot b}\,.

In this case we can use the delta function to write all the other scalar functions in terms of
q_{1}^{2}
,
q_{2}^{2}
and
q^{2}
, however we need to do the angular integral over the delta function itself. To this end we can define

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{D-2}}\frac{dq_{1}dq_{2}}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}\,\delta(q-q_{1}-q_{2})=4\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d|q_{1}|d|q_{2}|\,\phi(q_{1},q_{2},q)\,.

(220)

Using this and (
198
), we can compute the angular integrals in

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{D-2}}\frac{dq_{1}dq_{2}}{(2\pi)^{2(D-2)}}\,e^{ib\cdot(q_{1}+q_{2})}=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{D-2}}\frac{dq_{1}dq_{2}dq}{(2\pi)^{2(D-2)}}\,\delta(q-q_{1}-q_{2})\,e^{ib\cdot q}\,,

(221)

to find the flat space version of (
162
)

\omega_{q_{1}}(b)\,\omega_{q_{2}}(b)=2\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}d|q|\,\phi(q_{1},q_{2},q)\,\omega_{q}(b)\,.

(222)

Using the explicit expressions for
\Phi
and
\phi
(which can be found for instance in appendix E of
Penedones:2010ue
), one can further check that under the flat space limit

R^{4-D}\Phi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\ \to\ \phi(q_{1},q_{2},q)\,.

(223)

With this relation is clear that the expressions in
(
219
) are indeed related by the flat space limit provided that the vertex functions are related by
