V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)\ \to\ V(q_{1},q_{2})=V(t_{1},t_{2},t)\,.

(224)

6.2
Constraining AdS quantities

We saw above that all elements of the impact parameter optical theorems in AdS and flat space are related by the flat space limit provided that
j(\nu)
,
\beta(\nu)
,
\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)
and
V\big(\nu_{1}^{2},\nu_{2}^{2},\nu^{2}\big)
are given by their flat space counterparts in the limit.
In this subsection, we briefly review how the limit actually constrains these functions. All of these objects depend on two dimensionless quantities, the spectral parameters
\nu
and the t’Hooft coupling
\lambda
. Let us discuss this for a generic function
f(\nu)
that is required to satisfy the flat space limit

f(\nu,\lambda)\,\to\,f(t)\,.

(225)

Existence of the gravity limit requires the function to have an expansion in negative powers of
\sqrt{\lambda}
of the form

f(\nu,\lambda)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{n}(\nu)}{\lambda^{n/2}}\,.

(226)

In order for the flat space limit (
203
) of the function to be finite, the functions
f_{n}(\nu)
must have an expansion in large
\nu
with leading power not larger than
2n
,

f_{n}(\nu)=a_{n,n}\nu^{2n}+a_{n,n-1}\nu^{2(n-1)}+a_{n,n-2}\nu^{2(n-2)}+\ldots\,,

(227)

which ensures finiteness of the limit order by order in the large
\lambda
expansion.
The flat space limit of
f(\nu,\lambda)
is then

f(\nu,\lambda)\ \to\ \sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n,n}\left(\frac{\nu^{2}}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)^{n}\to\ \sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n,n}(\alpha^{\prime}q^{2})^{n}\,.

(228)

At every order in
1/\sqrt{\lambda}
the leading power of
\nu
survives and is fixed by the flat space limit, while all the other powers are subleading, and cannot be determined from this condition.
These considerations hold for
j(\nu)
,
\beta(\nu)
,
\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)
and
V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)
, fixing part of these functions. These facts have been explored in detail for the functions
j(\nu)
and
\beta(\nu)
in
Cornalba:2008qf
;
Costa:2012cb
.

7
Relating type IIB string theory in AdS and flat space

Let us now apply our general ideas to a concrete example, the scattering of four dilatons in type IIB superstring theory on
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
. In the flat space limit this is related to
type IIB superstring theory on 10-dimensional flat space where the kinematics is restricted to the five dimensions arising from AdS. This happens since both the dilatons and the Pomerons are
R
-symmetry singlets, meaning the tidal excitations they couple to also have to be singlets.
As a consequence, the Regge limit does not probe the 10 dimensional nature of the string scattering process, as we consider only states with the vacuum quantum numbers associated to the compact manifold
S^{5}
.
For this case the discontinuity of the (finite
\alpha^{\prime}
) one-loop amplitude in the Regge limit was computed in
Amati:1987uf
and is precisely of the form (
90
) with
D=5
. The regime of validity of this description was discussed in detail in
Amati:1987uf
.
All we need to specify are the four dynamic quantities that we already discussed in the previous section. For the Regge trajectory and Pomeron propagator we have

j(t)=2+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\,t\,,\qquad\beta(t)=2\pi^{2}\frac{\Gamma\big(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{4}t\big)}{\Gamma\big(1+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{4}t\big)}\,e^{-\frac{i\pi\alpha^{\prime}}{4}t}\,.

(229)

As discussed in section
3.3
the vertex function can be obtained from the scattering amplitude of two dilatons and two Pomerons. This amplitude was computed in

Amati:1987uf
and reads

A^{12P_{1}P_{2}}(k,q_{1},q_{2})=-\frac{\Gamma(1+\alpha^{\prime}q_{12}/2)\,\Gamma\big(-\alpha^{\prime}\frac{k^{2}}{4}-\alpha^{\prime}q_{12}/2\big)\,\Gamma\big(\alpha^{\prime}\frac{k^{2}}{4}\big)}{2\Gamma(-\alpha^{\prime}q_{12}/2)\,\Gamma\big(1+\alpha^{\prime}q_{12}/2+\alpha^{\prime}\frac{k^{2}}{4}\big)\,\Gamma\big(1-\alpha^{\prime}\frac{k^{2}}{4}\big)}\,,

(230)

where
q_{12}=q_{1}\cdot q_{2}
.
This amplitude has poles at the masses (
m^{2}=4n/\alpha^{\prime}
) of the string states with residues

\underset{k^{2}=-4n/\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}}A^{12P_{1}P_{2}}(k,q_{1},q_{2})=\left(\frac{(-\alpha^{\prime}q_{12}/2)_{n}}{n!}\right)^{2}\,,\qquad n=0,1,2,\ldots\,,

(231)

and the resulting vertex function is given by (
89
)

V(q_{1},q_{2})=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{(-\alpha^{\prime}q_{12}/2)_{n}}{n!}\right)^{2}=\frac{\Gamma\big(1+\alpha^{\prime}\frac{t_{1}+t_{2}-t}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1+\alpha^{\prime}\frac{t_{1}+t_{2}-t}{4}\big)^{2}}\,.

(232)

Using the reasoning of section
6.2
, this immediately fixes the leading terms in
\nu,\nu_{i}
of the AdS vertex function at every order in
\lambda

V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)=\frac{\Gamma\Big(1-\frac{\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}-\nu^{2}}{2\sqrt{\lambda}}\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(1-\frac{\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}-\nu^{2}}{4\sqrt{\lambda}}\Big)^{2}}+\text{vanishing in flat space limit}\,.

(233)

Thus, the first two corrections from expanding (
233
) at large
\lambda
are

\displaystyle V(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu)

\displaystyle=1+\Big(0\cdot\big(\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}-\nu^{2}\big)+c_{1,0}\Big)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}

(234)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\pi^{2}}{96}\left(\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}-\nu^{2}\right)^{2}+c_{2,1}\big(\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}\big)+c^{\prime}_{2,1}\nu^{2}+c_{2,0}\right)\frac{1}{\lambda}+\ldots\,,

where we also included the constants that are not fixed by the flat space limit. We note that the constants multiplying the leading power of
\nu
at order
\big(\sqrt{\lambda}\big)^{-n}
have a uniform transcendentality of weight
n
, which can be seen by explicitly expanding (
233
). It would be interesting to understand the relation of this property with features of maximal transcendentality in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
Kotikov:2002ab
;
Kotikov:2007cy
.

Finally, all the spectral functions
\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)
of tree-level correlators that contribute to the optical theorem are constrained
by the flat space limit. Their flat space limit (
217
) is parameterized by on-shell three-point amplitudes in flat space.
These amplitudes are in principle encoded in the result (
231
),
which separates the contributions of particles with different masses, but not the ones of particles in different representations
\rho
.
The attempt to expand (
231
) into products of three-point amplitudes for different
\rho
and tensor structures
k
using (
88
) shows that this does not fully fix the
a_{m_{5},\rho_{5}}^{k}(t)
in (
97
) because the equations are quadratic.
However the three-point amplitudes can of course be computed in string theory, which is what the next subsection is about.
We will start with the 10D open superstring amplitudes of a massless vector, a Pomeron and an open string state up to mass level 2 which were computed in
DAppollonio:2013mgj
by studying string-brane scattering. These amplitudes have to be squared to obtain closed string amplitudes. Then the irreducible representations of the 10D massive Little group
SO(9)
have to be branched into irreducible representations of
SO(4)
to match with the CFT irreps and account for the fact that we have five non-compact dimensions.

7.1
Massive tree amplitudes in flat space

Now we will discuss the flat space three-point amplitudes that take part in the process and that will fix part of the tree-level correlators with external spinning legs in AdS via the flat space limit.
The goal is to derive the three-point amplitudes that appear in the unitarity cut (
231
) of the
four-point amplitude of two dilatons and two Pomerons (
230
). It will be convenient to consider the more general case of two gravitons instead of dilatons, with polarizations
\epsilon_{i}^{\mu\nu}=\epsilon_{i}^{\mu}\epsilon_{i}^{\nu}
, and obtain the dilaton amplitudes in the very end by replacing
\epsilon_{i}^{\mu\nu}
with
\eta^{\mu\nu}
.
By using explicitly transverse three-point amplitudes and the completeness relation (
92
), we can write tree-level unitarity (
88
)
in the form

\underset{k^{2}=-4n/\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}}A^{12P_{1}P_{2}}(k,q_{12})=\left(\frac{(-\alpha^{\prime}q_{12}/2)_{n}}{n!}\right)^{2}(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2})^{2}=\sum\limits_{\rho,i}A^{15P_{1}}_{n,\rho,i,\mathbf{m}}\pi^{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}_{\rho}A^{52P_{2}}_{n,\rho,i,\mathbf{n}}\,,

(235)

where for the massive levels, on which we will mostly focus,
\rho
is summed over irreducible representations of
SO(4)
and
i
is summed over degenerate states in the same representation.

Our starting point will be the open string three-point amplitudes of a massless vector, a Pomeron and an arbitrary massive state up to mass level 2 (we give some simpler explicit examples in Appendix
A
). These amplitudes were computed in
DAppollonio:2013mgj
by studying string-brane scattering.
Since in flat space there is no interaction between the left- and right-moving string modes, the closed string amplitudes factorize into products of open string amplitudes.
We can indeed check that the square root of the residues (
235
) matches the expansion in terms of the open string three-point amplitudes of
DAppollonio:2013mgj

\sqrt{\underset{k^{2}=-4n/\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}}A^{12P_{1}P_{2}}(k,q_{12})}=\frac{(-\alpha^{\prime}q_{12}/2)_{n}}{n!}(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2})=\sum\limits_{\rho_{L}}A^{15P_{1}}_{n,\rho_{L},\bm{\alpha}}\pi^{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma}}_{\rho_{L}}A^{52P_{2}}_{n,\rho_{L},\bm{\gamma}}\,.

(236)

We did this consistency check for the first three mass levels, for which
\rho_{L}
is summed over
the bosonic part (NS sector) of the chiral superstring spectrum in 10 dimensions, given by
Hanany:2010da

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=0:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}_{\,8}\,,

(237)

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=1:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}_{\,9}\oplus\,\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern In order to obtain three-point amplitudes for closed strings in
10D
, we need to square (
236
) and expand again in irreducible representations.
The first step is trivial

\underset{k^{2}=-4n/\alpha^{\prime}}{\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}}A^{12P_{1}P_{2}}(k,q_{12})=\sum\limits_{\rho_{L},\rho_{R}}A^{15P_{1}}_{n,\rho_{L},\bm{\alpha}}\ A^{15P_{1}}_{n,\rho_{R},\bm{\beta}}\ \pi^{\bm{\alpha},\bm{\gamma}}_{\rho_{L}}\ \pi^{\bm{\beta},\bm{\delta}}_{\rho_{R}}\ A^{52P_{2}}_{n,\rho_{L},\bm{\gamma}}\ A^{52P_{2}}_{n,\rho_{R},\bm{\delta}}\,,

(238)

however expanding this into irreducible representations requires some more work.
On an abstract level this is easily done in terms of the tensor product

\rho_{L}\otimes\rho_{R}=\bigoplus\limits_{\rho_{C}}\rho_{C}\,,

(239)

which can be computed explicitly in terms of characters using e.g. the WeylCharacterRing implementation in SageMath
sagemath
. For example, the closed string spectrum for the first two mass levels is

Tousethetensorproductinexplicitcalculationsrequiresconsiderablymoreworkandcanbedonebyformulating
(
239
)
asanequationintermsofprojectorstoirreduciblerepresentations

\pi_{\rho_{L}}^{\bm{\alpha};\bm{\gamma}}\pi_{\rho_{R}}^{\bm{\beta};\bm{\delta}}=\sum_{\rho_{C}\subset\rho_{L}\otimes\rho_{R}}p^{\bm{\alpha}\bm{\beta}}_{\rho_{L}\otimes\rho_{R}\to\rho_{C},\bm{\mu}}\pi_{\rho_{C}}^{\bm{\mu};\bm{\nu}}p^{\bm{\gamma}\bm{\delta}}_{\rho_{L}\otimes\rho_{R}\to\rho_{C},\bm{\nu}}\,.

(283)

5D
massive spin 2 field that also appears in the projection (
7.1
).
We alert the reader that whenever we write explicit amplitudes they are neither appropriately symmetrized nor traceless in order to write them more compactly. All explicit amplitudes should be understood as objects to be contracted with the projector for the associated representation.

from

height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}_{\,9}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule 690.4pt}}$}_{\,9}\oplus\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 690.4pt}}$}_{\,9}\oplus\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}_{\,9}\oplus\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}_{\,9}\oplus\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}_{\,9}\oplus\bullet_{\,9}\,.

To construct the relation (
283
) we need the projectors for all the representations in this list. They can be found in
Costa:2016hju
;
Costa:2018mcg
, however here we just remind the reader of two of the most familiar ones

\pi_{[2]_{d}}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2},\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}\eta^{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}+\eta^{\mu_{1}\nu_{2}}\eta^{\mu_{2}\nu_{1}}\big)-\frac{1}{d}\,\eta^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\eta^{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\,,\qquad\pi_{\bullet}=1\,,

(317)

and state that the closed string state
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}_{\,9}
comes in (
283
) with the tensor

p^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}_{[2]_{9}\otimes[2]_{9}\to[2]_{9},\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{36}{91}}\,\pi_{[2]_{9}}^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2};\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}\pi_{[2]_{9}}^{\beta_{1}\beta_{2};\delta_{1}\delta_{2}}\eta_{\gamma_{2}\delta_{1}}\eta_{\gamma_{1}\mu_{1}}\eta_{\delta_{1}\mu_{2}}\,,

(319)

where we introduced additional projectors contracted with metrics in order to have the correct index properties. This determines the following closed string amplitude via (
)

\displaystyle A^{15P_{1}}_{1,[2]_{9}\otimes[2]_{9}\to[2]_{9},\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{4\sqrt{91}}\Big[\big(q_{1,\mu_{1}}(\epsilon_{1,\mu_{2}}q_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1}+3q_{1,\mu_{2}})

(320)

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{1,\mu_{1}}(q_{1,\mu_{2}}q_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1}-3t_{1}\epsilon_{1,\mu_{2}})+v_{\mu_{1}}v_{\mu_{2}}(q_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1}){}^{2}\big)\Big]\,.

The branching rule for
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}_{\,9}
was already considered in (
7.1
). Using the projectors (
317
) it is easy to see that we can write explicitly

\pi_{[2]_{9}}^{a_{1}a_{2},b_{1}b_{2}}=\delta^{a_{1}}_{m_{1}}\delta^{a_{2}}_{m_{2}}\pi_{[2]_{4}}^{m_{1}m_{2},n_{1}n_{2}}\delta^{b_{1}}_{n_{1}}\delta^{b_{2}}_{n_{2}}+\frac{5}{36}\,\delta^{a_{1}a_{2}}\pi_{\bullet}\delta^{b_{1}b_{2}}\,,

(322)

from which we read off

b^{a_{1}a_{2}}_{[2]_{9}\to[2]_{4},m_{1}m_{2}}=\delta^{a_{1}}_{m_{1}}\delta^{a_{2}}_{m_{2}}\,,\qquad b^{a_{1}a_{2}}_{[2]_{9}\to\bullet_{4}}=\sqrt{\frac{5}{36}}\,\delta^{a_{1}a_{2}}\,.

(323)

Finally, inserting (
320
) and (
323
) into (
) we compute the
5D
closed string amplitude

A^{15P_{1}}_{1,[2]_{9}\otimes[2]_{9}\to[2]_{9}\to\bullet_{4}}=\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\frac{5}{91}}\alpha^{\prime}\left((q_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1})^{2}-2t_{1}\right)

(324)

We derive the complete list of such level 1 three-point amplitudes in appendices
B
and
C
.

7.2
Constraints on spinning AdS amplitudes

In this section we use the flat-space string amplitudes to constrain the high-energy, tree-level AdS
5
amplitudes with two dilatons and two spinning operators
\langle\phi\phi\mathcal{O}_{5}\mathcal{O}_{6}\rangle
. Since the operators in question are of stringy nature (i.e. the bulk fields have
m^{2}\sim 4n/\alpha^{\prime}
for a very large AdS radius), their dimensions grow with the ’t Hooft coupling (
\Delta\sim\lambda^{1/4}
) and transform in the
SO(4)
representations discussed above in the flat space case. This means that generically
\mathcal{O}_{5}
and
\mathcal{O}_{6}
are in bosonic mixed-symmetry representations.

As discussed in section
6.1
, the spectral functions that determine the spinning AdS correlators (
158
) via (
159
) are determined in the flat space limit by the three-point amplitudes (
97
)

\lambda^{\frac{j(\nu)-1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{|\rho_{5}|-k_{5}}{4}}\lambda^{\frac{|\rho_{6}|-k_{6}}{4}}\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)\ \to\ \frac{1}{2\pi}\,a_{m_{5},\rho_{5}}^{k_{5}}(t)\,a_{m_{6},\rho_{6}}^{k_{6}}(t)\beta(t)\,.

(325)

In other words, the leading term in
\nu
at each order in
\lambda
is fixed by the flat space expression (see section
6.2
)

\lambda^{\frac{j(\nu)-1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{|\rho_{5}|-k_{5}}{4}}\lambda^{\frac{|\rho_{6}|-k_{6}}{4}}\beta^{k_{5},k_{6}}_{(\Delta_{5},\rho_{5}),(\Delta_{6},\rho_{6})}(\nu)=\frac{1}{2\pi}a_{m_{5},\rho_{5}}^{k_{5}}(t)\,a_{m_{6},\rho_{6}}^{k_{6}}(t)\beta(t)\Big|_{\alpha^{\prime}t=-\frac{\nu^{2}}{\sqrt{\lambda}}}+\ldots\,,

(326)

where
\ldots
are terms that vanish in the flat space limit.
Let us now use this to study some specific examples. We begin with the example of section
7.1.1
where we computed an amplitude involving a graviton, a Pomeron and a particular scalar at mass level 1 in (
324
).
In this result
\epsilon_{1,\mu}
is such that
\epsilon_{\mu\nu}=\epsilon_{1,\mu}\epsilon_{1,\nu}
parametrizes a general graviton polarization which must be replaced by the metric
\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow\eta_{\mu\nu}
to obtain the dilaton amplitude

A_{1,[2]_{9}\otimes[2]_{9}\rightarrow[2]_{9}\rightarrow\bullet_{4}}^{D5P_{1}}=a_{4/\alpha^{\prime},[2]_{9}\otimes[2]_{9}\rightarrow[2]_{9}\rightarrow\bullet_{4}}^{0}(t)=-\frac{3}{8}\sqrt{\frac{5}{91}}\,\alpha^{\prime}t\,,

(327)

where we used that
(q_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1})^{2}=-t_{1}
for the dilaton case.
Thus, for the AdS correlator of this particular scalar and three dilatons we have

\beta^{0,0}_{(2\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}}+\ldots,\bullet),(4,\bullet)}(\nu)=\frac{3}{8}\sqrt{\frac{5}{91}}\frac{\nu^{2}}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\,\beta(\nu)+\text{vanishing in the flat space limit}\,.

(328)

Note that with respect to the case of four dilaton scattering we have an extra power of
1/\sqrt{\lambda}
, so the term of order
\lambda^{0}
is absent, confirming that tidal excitations are suppressed at large
\lambda
, which in turn agrees with the considerations of
Meltzer:2019pyl
(we verified this fact for all amplitudes at level 1).
In particular, this is consistent with the large
\lambda
suppression of
c_{\phi_{1}\phi_{2}j(\nu)}
for non-identical scalars, since our stringy mode is certainly different from the dilaton. Such a suppression is not a priori obvious from writing a bulk interaction between two different scalars and a spin
J
field (to be Sommerfeld-Watson transformed into a Pomeron), which makes this a non-trivial realization of the bounds derived in
Costa:2017twz
;
Meltzer:2017rtf
.
This example is particularly simple, since there is a unique three-point structure in the case of the scalar.

More generally, we can consider amplitudes with several tensor structures constructed from
v_{a}
’s and
q_{a}
’s (equivalently,
\widehat{p}
and
\nabla_{p}
in AdS). Let us take as a representative example, the case of the spin 4 operator at level 1. This operator is typically used to define
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
and sits in the leading Regge trajectory. The corresponding graviton-Pomeron-spin 4 amplitude was worked out in Appendices
B
and
C
, and reads

A_{[2]_{9}\otimes[2]_{9}\rightarrow[4]_{9}\rightarrow[4]_{4}}^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}a_{4}}(\epsilon_{1})=\frac{1}{8}\alpha^{\prime}\big(2\epsilon_{1,a_{1}}q_{1,a_{2}}+v_{a_{1}}v_{a_{2}}q_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\big)\big(2\epsilon_{1,a_{3}}q_{1,a_{4}}+v_{a_{3}}v_{a_{4}}q_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{1}\big)\,.

(329)

We again emphasize that it is understood that the amplitude should be contracted with
\pi_{[4]_{4}}^{\bm{\alpha};\bm{\beta}}
. Furthermore, we are using the simplifying transverse kinematics discussed above. This means that upon doing the dilaton replacement, we have

A_{[2]_{9}\otimes[2]_{9}\rightarrow[4]_{9}\rightarrow[4]_{4}}^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}a_{4}}=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{8}\big(-t_{1}v_{a_{1}}v_{a_{2}}v_{a_{3}}v_{a_{4}}+4v_{a_{1}}v_{a_{2}}q_{1,a_{3}}q_{1,a_{4}}\big)\,,

(330)

where we used the symmetry of the indices and note that transverse kinematics ensures that the term proportional to
\epsilon_{1,a_{1}}\epsilon_{1,a_{2}}
gets mapped to a transverse metric which is annihilated by the projector to
[4]_{4}
.
In this case we can directly use (
216
) to match both tensor structures at once

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{(2\lambda^{\frac{1}{4}}+\ldots,[4]_{4}),(4,\bullet)}_{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}a_{4}}(\nu)={}

\displaystyle\frac{\beta(\nu)}{8\sqrt{\lambda}}\big(\nu^{2}\widehat{p}_{a_{1}}\widehat{p}_{a_{2}}\widehat{p}_{a_{3}}\widehat{p}_{a_{4}}+4\widehat{p}_{a_{1}}\widehat{p}_{a_{2}}\nabla_{p\,a_{3}}\nabla_{p\,a_{4}}\big)

(331)

\displaystyle+\text{vanishing in flat space limit}\,.

We again note that these corrections are suppressed at large
\lambda
.

8
Conclusions

In this work, we derived a perturbative CFT optical theorem which computes the dDisc of a correlator in the
1/N
expansion in terms of single discontinuities of lower order correlators. Notably, this allows the determination of double-trace contributions to a given one-loop holographic correlator, even when the intermediate fields have spin, which makes them much harder to handle using unitarity formulas in terms of the CFT data. This also clarifies the underlying CFT principles behind cutting formulas for AdS Witten diagrams, which so far used bulk quantities
Meltzer:2019nbs
;
Meltzer:2020qbr
.

Using the perturbative CFT optical theorem we fixed the form of the AdS one-loop four-point scattering amplitude in the high-energy limit, accounting for the physical effect of tidal excitations. This corresponds to box Witten diagrams with two-Pomeron exchange and general string fields as intermediate states. To do this, we transformed the optical theorem to CFT impact parameter space, in which the loop level phase shift is obtained as a contraction of tree-level phase shifts.
Using the general structure of spinning correlators in the s-channel Regge limit, we rewrote all the tidal excitations in terms of a single scalar function, the AdS vertex function.

For the case of
{\cal N}=4
SYM, dual to type IIB strings, we fixed part of the answer by relating our expression to the flat space results of
Amati:1987wq
;
Amati:1987uf
;
Amati:1988tn
for high energy string scattering, requiring consistency with the flat space limit in impact parameter space. This procedure fixes part of the AdS vertex function and therefore also part of the CFT correlation function at one-loop in the Regge limit.
Additionally, interpreting the previous result in terms of unitarity, we used the flat-space behavior to constrain the spectral function for certain spinning CFT correlators at tree level in the Regge limit.

There are several open directions and applications of this work.
First, we emphasize that the CFT optical theorem is quite general and does not rely on AdS ingredients.
Moreover, it works directly at the level of correlators instead of having to extract the CFT data, which is very difficult to resum into correlators. It would be interesting to test and use this formula for more general holographic correlators and, since the expansion parameter does not necessarily need to be
1/N
, in weakly coupled CFTs such as
\phi^{4}
theory at the Wilson-Fisher fixed point in
4-\epsilon
dimensions.

Another playground to apply our gluing formula is
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM at weak t’Hooft coupling in the Regge limit. One could try to derive the order
1/N^{4}
correlator
explicitly at leading order in
\lambda
, using the techniques introduced in
Cornalba:2008qf
. The corresponding double discontinuity should be the square
of order
1/N^{2}
correlators with impact factors that include the intermediate states.

In the Regge limit there are kinematical conditions in the CFT optical theorem that simplified the integrations over Lorentzian configurations. An interesting generalization would be to systematically study kinematic corrections to the Regge limit. In fact, in the recent work
Caron-Huot:2020nem
the authors derived a Regge expansion for the correlator valid for any boost. It would be interesting to see how to incorporate this into our analysis, both in a general structural way, but also potentially to impose specific constraints from the flat space limit in a more general kinematic setup. A possible extension of this work is to consider a higher number of bulk loops. This was analyzed in the large
\Delta_{\text{gap}}
limit in
Meltzer:2019pyl
. Let us give a few concrete ideas for the stringy generalization of that analysis.
The leading contribution in the Regge limit at
k-1
loops is expected to be
k
-Pomeron exchange, related to a
k
-fold product of tree-level phase shifts.
By repeatedly using (
162
) one can define a generalization of the function
\Phi
for such a product, so we expect that the contribution of intermediate states can again be expressed by a vertex function
13

13

This corresponds to eikonalization in the operator sense of
Amati:1987uf
where the phase shift is an operator in the string Hilbert space, with matrix elements between all possible string states.

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\mathcal{B}_{k-1}(S,L)=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\nu\left(\prod\limits_{n=1}^{k}d\nu_{n}\,\beta^{(*)}(\nu_{n})\right)

\displaystyle V(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{k},\nu)^{2}\,S^{\sum_{m}j(\nu_{m})-k}

(332)

\displaystyle\Phi(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{k},\nu)\,\Omega_{i\nu}(L)\,,

where the product of
\beta(\nu_{n})
must be real, which means that the answer is slightly different depending on whether the number of loops is even or odd
Meltzer:2019pyl
.

V_{k}(q_{1},\dots,q_{k})=\int\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{d\sigma_{i}}{2\pi}\prod_{1\leq j<l\leq k}|e^{i\sigma_{j}}-e^{i\sigma_{l}}|^{\alpha^{\prime}q_{l}\cdot q_{j}}\,.

Note that the symmetry of the integrand is such that only

integrals are non-trivial. For example, the one-loop case just gives
